Dear Secretary Reese, 
In accordance with Section 205 of the Federal Power Act (“FPA”)1 and Part 35 of the 
Commission’s regulations,2 the New York Independent System Operator, Inc. (“NYISO”) 
submits proposed revisions to its Open-Access Transmission Tariff (“OATT”) and Market 
Administration and Control Area Services Tariff (“Services Tariff”).3  The proposed revisions 
establish new Large Load Integration Procedures (“LLIP”) and related market participation 
provisions.  They are intended to fully satisfy the concerns raised by the Commission in its 
Order Instituting Proceeding Under Section 206 of the Federal Power Act (“Show Cause 
Order”)4 to the extent that they involve issues that are within the scope of the NYISO’s tariff 
filing authority.5   
The proposals in this filing are designed to account for issues and conditions in the 
NYISO region.  They build on the NYISO’s recently adopted Standard Interconnection 
Procedures and on various NYISO-specific independent entity variations that are foundational to 
the interconnection process in New York and that bring many benefits.  They also: (i) address the 
unprecedented near-term resource adequacy challenges, driven in large part by an expected 
influx of large loads, that the NYISO now faces; (ii) leverage the NYISO’s existing “financial” 
transmission reservation service model to maximize the benefits to flexible large loads and other 
customers; and (iii) contain various mechanisms to protect customers.    
 
The NYISO’s proposals recognize that NYISO is a single-state entity whose operational 
boundaries coincide with the retail rate and resource adequacy jurisdiction of the New York State

Public Service Commission (“NYPSC”).  The NYPSC has separately launched a proceeding to 
address state-jurisdictional interconnection reforms that are likely to influence the evolution of 
Commission-jurisdictional large load rules in New York.6   
 
 
The NYISO’s proposal incorporates a compromise approach to resource adequacy 
requirements, the State Exempt Load Project exception, that is intended to reconcile the 
NYISO’s reliability responsibilities with the NYPSC’s resource adequacy authority in a manner 
that is consistent with cooperative federalism principles.7  More generally, the NYISO has 
carefully considered and sought to adhere to the Commission’s guidance on large load 
jurisdictional issues and to respect both federal and state authority while still producing a 
proposal that effectively meets the NYISO region’s needs. 
 
Parts II and III of this transmittal letter describe the NYISO’s proposed new LLIP and 
explain its benefits.  Among other things, Parts II and III: (i) describe the NYISO’s proposed 
definition of “Large Load,” which reflects minor changes to the Show Cause Order’s noted 
version to reflect NYISO’s circumstances; (ii) incorporate clear and detailed large load 
interconnection rules into the NYISO OATT; (iii) introduce transition rules that balance the 
timely implementation of the new procedures while avoiding disruptions to existing commercial 
arrangements that have achieved reasonable progress; (iv) promote timely Large Load 
interconnections; (v) provide for new types of Commission-jurisdictional transmission services, 
i.e, “Firm Withdrawal Service” and “Non-Firm Withdrawal Service”, that reflect the unique 
characteristics of both the NYISO’s transmission model and of co-located Large Loads, Large 
Load with behind the meter generation, and flexible Large Loads that are able to limit their use 
of the system; (vi) delineate the division of responsibilities between the NYISO and 
transmission-owning utilities under the LLIP; (vii) include detailed application and readiness 
requirements to deter speculative projects that could unduly shift costs onto existing customers; 
(viii) establish a Large Load Integration Study Process; (ix) address the identification and cost 
allocation of upgrades; and (x) describe how alternative transmission technologies will be 
considered.   
Part IV of this transmittal letter describes the various new market participation provisions 
that the NYISO is proposing to account for the proliferation of Large Loads and to support its 
Large Load reforms.  These proposals include revised and new market rules to govern: (i) Large 
Loads eligible to be served via Firm Withdrawal Service, (ii) Large Loads or portions thereof 
and an “Associated Resource(s)” that enter into a “Co-Located Arrangement” or “Partner 
Resource Arrangement” so that the Large Load may be served via some amount of Firm 
Withdrawal Service; (iii) Large Loads or portions thereof served via Non-Firm Withdrawal 
Service and/or are designated Zero Withdrawal Service Loads; (iv) the co-location of Large 
Loads with Supply.

Within this framework, the NYISO would, among other things: (i) establish a rule under 
which Non-Firm Withdrawal Service would be curtailed when the applicable nodal Locational-
Based Marginal Price (“LBMP”) for a Large Load reaches $200 MW/h; (ii) explain how 
different categories of Large Loads will interact with the NYISO’s capacity market Demand Side 
Resource rules; (iii) subject all Large Loads to fees and charges on a gross load basis; (iv) adopt 
a System Reliability Benefit Fee applicable to Large Loads served by some amount of Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service to allocate costs to them that are roughly commensurate with the benefits 
they receive from ICAP Resources and the value of the reliability services that they provide; (v) 
apply certain data reporting requirements to all Large Loads; (vi) introduce a new Co-Located 
and Limited Load Configuration market participation model; (vii) use Associated Resource rules 
tied to NYISO Capacity Regions in lieu of the Show Cause Order’s “electrically proximate 
resource;” (viii) address the extent to which Demand Side Resources and Aggregations may 
become Associated Resources; (ix) enhance the NYISO’s DER and Aggregation participation 
model rules; (x) sunset the NYISO’s existing BTM:NG Resource program; and (xi) update the 
existing “buyer-side” capacity market power mitigation measures to reflect new issues presented 
by Large Loads and emerging resource adequacy challenges in New York.    
Part V explains why the NYISO is not proposing to adopt the three new transmission 
services that the Commission established in the PJM Co-Location Order8 and provides additional 
information regarding the benefits of the NYISO’s established financial transmission reservation 
model and new Withdrawal Services.    
New York’s Commission-jurisdictional transmission-owning utilities are concurrently 
making separate FPA Section 205 filings to address Show Cause Order matters that fall under 
their reserved filing rights.  As discussed in Part VI of this transmittal letter, the traditional New 
York Transmission Owners (“NYTOs”)9 are making a contemporaneous FPA Section 205 filing 
that will propose various tariff revisions to the NYISO OATT, principally to Rate Schedule 5, 
and add several new pro forma agreements to the NYISO OATT.  Among other things the 
NYTOs will submit a new two-party pro forma cost recovery agreement to address the cost-
shifting issues discussed in the Show Cause Order.  The non-incumbent transmission owners 
(“NTOs”)10 that were subject to the Show Cause Order will be making concurrent Section 205 
filings to address matters that implicate their reserved filing rights and that reflect the structural 
differences between them and the NYTOs.  The NYISO believes that its Section 205 filing, in 
combination with the NYTO’s and NTOs’, will address all of the issues raised by the Show 
Cause Order.11

Part VII explains that the Commission should determine that this filing is just, 
reasonable, and not unduly discriminatory and issue an order accepting it under the FPA Section 
205 standard of review without requiring any modifications or additional procedures.  At the 
same time, the NYISO consents to the Commission’s exercise of its authority to modify or sever 
any element of the NYISO’s proposed tariff revisions to the maximum extent permitted by law.   
As noted below in Part VIII, the NYISO’s proposal has evolved substantially in response 
to extensive stakeholder feedback.  The NYISO’s proposed tariff revisions received the super-
majority stakeholder approval required under the NYISO’s shared governance model to 
authorize its submission under FPA Section 205.    
Part IX below explains that the NYISO is requesting a February 1, 2027 effective date for 
the LLIP-related tariff provisions described in Parts II and III, June 1, 2028 for the Large Load 
market participation provisions set forth in Part IV, and May 1, 2029 for the sunsetting of the 
existing BTM:NG Resource program which is described in Part IV.I.       
Consistent with the  August 14, 2026 letter order (“Abeyance Order”)12 in the Show 
Cause Order proceeding, the NYISO respectfully requests in Part X below that the Commission 
hold the Section 206 proceedings initiated by the Show Cause Order in continued abeyance 
pending action on this filing.  If the Commission accepts this filing it should also issue an order 
terminating the Show Cause Order proceeding with respect to the NYISO.13   
Finally, the NYISO asks that the Commission briefly extend the comment period on this 
filing from the standard twenty-one days to twenty-eight days. As noted in Part XI a modest 
extension would provide interested parties with additional time to address the numerous, 
complex, and consequential issues implicated by the NYISO’s proposals and by the Show Cause 
Order.  A one-week extension would also accommodate the Thanksgiving holiday and should 
not hinder the Commission’s review because the NYISO’s earliest requested effective date is 
more than two weeks after the end of the standard sixty-day notice period.

II. 
THE NYISO’S PROPOSED TARIFF REVISIONS TO ESTABLISH AND 
IMPLEMENT LARGE LOAD INTEGRATION PROCEDURES 
A. 
Large Load 
In the Show Cause Order, the Commission found that it would be reasonable to define a 
large load as “a new commercial or industrial customer, located at a single site behind one or 
more points of interconnection, and that has a peak load of 50 MW or greater and interconnects 
to the transmission system at a voltage level of greater than 69 kV, and is not part of a co-
location arrangement.”14  The Commission stated that an “appropriate definition, including a 
load size and interconnection voltage threshold, should be based on the characteristics of 
NYISO’s transmission system.”15 
The NYISO proposes to adopt the Commission’s definition of Large Load with certain 
variations described below.  In particular, the NYISO proposes to define Large Load for its 
OATT and Services Tariff as follows: 
Large Load shall mean a commercial or industrial Load located at a single site or 
in close proximity, behind one or more Points of Interconnection, that has a peak 
Load of 50 MW or greater connected to the New York State Transmission 
System, which is subject to and has completed the applicable requirements of the 
Large Load Integration Procedures in Attachment LL of the ISO OATT.  For 
purposes of this definition, Load shall be considered to be located “in close 
proximity” if it: (i) is located within 2 miles and 2 electrical buses and (ii) is 
electrically located in the same Capacity Region.16 
First, the NYISO proposes to provide that the Load may be located either at a single site 
or in close proximity.  As indicated in the definition, the NYISO proposes to define “in close 
proximity” to mean that the Load is both: (i) located within 2 miles and 2 electrical buses and (ii) 
is electrically located in the same Capacity Region.  Load projects could be spread across a 
geographic area that necessitate more than one Point of Interconnection (e.g., data center 
campus) and should be addressed as a single project in assessing their system impacts.  The 
requirement for both 2 miles and 2 buses constitutes a reasonable basis for defining close 
proximity based on the different topography across the New York State Transmission System, 
as: (i) there are parts of the system in which 2 buses are located many miles away from each 
other (e.g. upstate New York), and (ii) additionally, there are parts of the system in which 
numerous buses can be located within 2 miles (e.g. New York City).  In addition, it is necessary 
that a single Large Load be located within a single Capacity Region for purposes of the 
 
14 Show Cause Order at P 64. 
15 Id.

of Large Load)

assessment of the reliability impacts of the Large Load, including accounting for any Associated

Second, the NYISO proposes not to exclude load that is part of a co-location arrangement 
as in the Commission’s definition.  The NYISO proposes to establish specific requirements 
concerning Co-Located Arrangements as described in Part IV.D. 
Finally, for purposes of the transition requirements for load projects currently 
participating in the NYISO’s existing load interconnection process, the NYISO proposes to 
clarify that the requirements applicable to Large Loads in its tariffs apply to those load projects 
that were subject to and completed the requirements in the Large Load Integration Procedures.   
B. 
Proposed New Transmission Services for Large Loads 
The NYISO’s proposed rules for the participation of Large Loads in the ISO 
Administered Markets vary depending on a Large Load’s Withdrawal Service(s)17 
classification(s) as determined in the LLIP.18  Depending on the results of the LLIP, a Large 
Load may be eligible to receive Firm Withdrawal Service19 and/or Non-Firm Withdrawal 
Service20 and/or to be a Zero Withdrawal Service Load.21  
The new Firm and Non-Firm Withdrawal Services are described in new Section 3A of the 
NYISO OATT.  They are new forms of Commission-jurisdictional Transmission Service with 
terms and conditions that reflect the unique operational and reliability challenges that providing 
transmission service to Large Loads presents to the NYISO system.  These challenges are 
described in the Altman Affidavit.  In addition, as described in Part V below and in the Harvey 
Affidavit the design of new Firm and Non-Firm Withdrawal Services is consistent with the 
NYISO’s existing financial reservation transmission service model.  The creation of the new 
Withdrawal Services is responsive to the Show Cause Order’s preliminary finding that the 
NYISO’s tariff appears to be unjust and unreasonable because it does not include transmission 
services for Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of Eligible Load, or on 
 
17 See proposed Services Tariff § 2.23. 
18 All Large Loads that are subject to the Large Load Integration Procedures as described in Part II.D.i must 
complete the procedures set forth in Attachment LL of the OATT and meet the requirements, as applicable, to 
receive Transmission Service under the OATT.  See proposed Services Tariff § 4.1.6. 
19 “Firm Withdrawal Service” shall be defined as “Transmission Service offered to a Large Load, or a 
portion thereof, that does not cause adverse impacts to resource adequacy and/or transmission security.”  See 
proposed OATT Attach. LL § 44.1 (definition of Firm Withdrawal Service). 
20 “Non-Firm Withdrawal Service” shall be defined as “Transmission Service subject to dispatch

adequacy and/or transmission security.”  See proposed OATT Attach. LL § 44.1 (definition of Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service).  The NYISO would also revise the definition of “Transmission Service” in existing OATT 
§1.20 to account for and to reference the creation of the new “Withdrawal Services.”

behalf of flexible large loads that are willing and able to limit their use of the transmission 
system under certain conditions.22   
For convenience, this transmittal letter sometimes refers informally to the NYISO 
providing Firm or Non-Firm Withdrawal Service “to” Large Loads or to Large Loads “taking” or 
“receiving” them.  Such references are meant to encompass instances where a Transmission 
Customer is taking the services on a Large Load’s behalf in which a Large Load is acting as its 
own Load-Serving Entity.  They should not be construed as indicating that Firm or Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Services are not Commission-jurisdictional forms of transmission service.     
Firm Withdrawal Service would be available to Large Loads that are determined, through 
the LLIP, not to cause adverse impacts or violations to resource adequacy or transmission 
security or which adverse impacts or violations are mitigated as described in Part II.G.i.23   
A Large Load, or any portion thereof, taking Firm Withdrawal Service will be able to use 
the transmission system to serve its load in a price-agnostic manner and will be treated in the 
same manner as other customers taking firm Transmission Service, including with respect to 
curtailment priority.  Firm Withdrawal Service will be subject to NYISO emergency operating 
procedures, and Large Loads taking such service may be required to curtail for reliability needs 
consistent with the priority treatment that load resources receive today.24  All requests for Firm 
Withdrawal Service will have the same reservation priority, and Firm Withdrawal Service will 
have the same priority as Firm Point-to-Point Transmission Service.  Firm Withdrawal Service 
will have priority over Non-Firm Withdrawal Service.25 
Non-Firm Withdrawal Service would be made available to dispatchable Large Loads that 
are deemed to cause violations or adverse impacts to resource adequacy and/or transmission 
security, which violations or adverse impacts are not mitigated as described in Part II.G.i.26  
Large Loads taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service will be eligible to withdraw Energy from the 
transmission system, subject to a bid cap and other necessary requirements.27  As described in 
Part IV.B.ii.b, the NYISO proposes to use a mandatory bid cap of $200/MWh as the mechanism 
to curtail Non-Firm Large Loads to avoid adverse impacts to the system.  Non-Firm Withdrawal 
Service will be subject to NYISO dispatch and emergency operating procedures.28  
Zero Withdrawal Service Loads will be either (a) a Limited Withdrawal Service Load in 
a Co-Located and Limited Load Configuration (“CLLC”), or (b) a Co-Located Located Load in a 
Co-Located Local Generator Configuration (“CLGC”).  Zero Withdrawal Service Loads will be 
Large Loads that have not requested Withdrawal Service rights and will not be authorized to 
withdraw Energy from the NYS Transmission System.  As described in Part IV.B.ii.e, a Zero

C. 
Large Load Integration Procedures 
In the Show Cause Order, the Commission found that, notwithstanding the NYISO’s 
existing load interconnection requirements, the NYISO’s existing OATT appears to be unjust 
and unreasonable because it does not include provisions that address with sufficient clarity and 
consistency the study process for the provision of transmission service to Eligible Customers on 
behalf of large loads, including the unique operational characteristics of large loads.29   
The NYISO proposes to establish new Large Load Integration Procedures, which will be 
located in Attachment LL to the OATT.30  The procedures include clear and detailed 
requirements pursuant to which: (i) an Eligible Customer may submit a Large Load Integration 
Request for the evaluation of the proposed interconnection of a Large Load Project to the New 
York State Transmission System and (ii) the NYISO and Transmission Owners, as applicable, 
will evaluate the proposed interconnection, identify any Attachment Facilities and Network 
Upgrades required for the reliable interconnection of such project and their estimated costs and 
schedule, and determine the project’s Firm Withdrawal Service or Non-Firm Withdrawal Service 
amounts or, if requested, its eligibility to proceed as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load. 
The NYISO highlights in Part II.C.i key elements of its proposed Large Load Integration 
Procedures.  The NYISO then provides a high-level overview of the new procedures in Part 
II.C.ii and in the figure in Attachment VI.  Finally, the NYISO describes the new procedures in 
detail in Parts II.D through III, including demonstrating that the procedures are just and 
reasonable and address the findings of the Show Cause Order in light of the NYISO’s distinct 
planning framework and market structure, its role as a single-state independent system operator 
(“ISO”), and other New York-specific conditions.  The proposed procedures are supported by the 
affidavit of Thinh T. Nguyen, NYISO Senior Manager of Interconnection Projects (“Nguyen 
Affidavit”). 
i. 
Key Elements of the Large Load Integration Procedures 
a) 
Clear and Detailed Procedures  
The NYISO proposes in this filing to establish new Large Load Integration Procedures as 
described in detail in Parts II.D through III that set forth clear and detailed tariff requirements 
concerning the application for and the study of the proposed interconnection of Large Load 
Projects.  The NYISO proposes that these new procedures replace its existing load 
interconnection requirements set forth in Sections 3.9 and 4.5 of the OATT, subject to certain 
transition requirements.  
b) 
Effective Date and Transition Process 
 
29 Show Cause Order at PP 3, 46. 
30 See proposed OATT Attach. LL § 44.1 (definition of Large Load Integration Procedures).

PDF page 14

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 14 
 
 
The NYISO proposes that the Large Load Integration Procedures have an effective date 
of February 1, 2027, as described in Parts II.D.ii and IX.  The NYISO also proposes to establish 
transition rules that balance timely implementing the new requirements adopted in response to 
Show Cause Order with minimizing disruptions to those projects that have already completed or 
progressed to certain defined milestones in the NYISO’s existing load interconnection process.  
As described in Part II.D.ii, the NYISO proposes to establish two transition groups – Transition 
Group A and Transition Group B – for projects that meet the definition of Large Load and satisfy 
certain tariff-prescribed milestones and requirements.  Projects in Transition Group A must have 
advanced at least as far as the facilities study stage under the existing rules by a tariff-prescribed 
deadline and can elect to complete the load interconnection process under the NYISO’s existing 
rules.  Projects in Transition Group B must have advanced at least as far as the completion of the 
system impact study by a tariff-prescribed deadline and can elect to enter the first Large Load 
Integration Study Process under the new Attachment LL rules, which transition process will not 
require the performance of a System Impact Study.  Finally, the NYISO proposes additional 
transition rules to facilitate the completion of certain ongoing system impact studies for 
information purposes as described in Part II.D.ii.  
The NYISO’s proposed transition framework—under which it seeks to balance parties’ 
expectations based on their relative progress against the proposal’s benefits and the need for 
administrable deadlines—is supported by ample precedent. Market participants do not have a 
reasonable expectation that market rules will remain unchanged.31 To the contrary, the 
Commission has stated that “there is always risk that tariffs and regulations can change, which is 
a risk that market participants take in a competitive market.”32 Consequently, where, as here, 
revisions in a Section 205 proposal are filed prospectively, such revisions do not implicate the 
filed rate doctrine or rule against retroactive ratemaking.33 Rather, the Commission balances any 
settled expectations against the benefits of the proposal, and has accepted the revisions where the 
benefits outweigh such expectations.34 As discussed in greater detail below, the NYISO’s 
proposal reasonably accounts for parties’ expectations by establishing transition rules based on 
each project’s relative stage of development, demonstrated commercial readiness, and size.35 
This is consistent with the Commission’s directive to reasonably consider existing commercial 
arrangements while drawing clear, administrable lines for the proposal’s effectiveness. 
c) 
Timeframe of Large Load Integration Procedures  
The NYISO proposes a timeframe for the performance of the Large Load Integration 
Study Process of between one year and six months and two years, including a 90-day period for 
the performance of the System Impact Study, as described in Part II.E.ii  The overall duration 
depends on when a Large Load Project satisfies the application requirements for entry into the

study.  The timeframe constitutes a just and reasonable period of time for the NYISO, 
Transmission Owners, and Eligible Customers to perform their respective responsibilities under 
the proposed Large Load Integration Study Process as described in Part II.E.ii.  These 
responsibilities include performing preliminary analyses and assessing the feasibility of proposed 
interconnections, studying the proposed interconnection of the Group of projects participating in 
the study, including their impacts on the resource adequacy and transmission security of the 
transmission system, identifying any Attachment Facilities and Network Upgrades required for 
the reliable interconnection of such project, including determining and allocating the cost 
estimates for such facilities, and determining the project’s Firm Withdrawal Service or Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service amounts or, if requested, its eligibility to proceed as a Zero Withdrawal 
Service Load.   
d) 
Role of Transmission Owners 
The NYISO proposes to allocate the scope of responsibilities in the Large Load 
Integration Procedures between itself and the Transmission Owners as detailed in this filing 
letter.  The proposed allocation is similar to the allocation of responsibilities in the NYISO’s 
existing load interconnection procedures.  It accounts for the NYISO’s and Transmission 
Owners’ respective planning responsibilities, system knowledge, and access to underlying 
criteria and data.  The parallel division of work will also allow the NYISO and the Transmission 
Owners, working in parallel, to meet the proposed study timeframes and minimize inefficient 
and duplicative analyses. 
e) 
Assessment of Reliability Impacts and Determinations of 
Withdrawal Service 
The NYISO proposes that the interconnection of Large Loads must not adversely impact 
resource adequacy or transmission security.  To assess the impacts of Large Loads on the 
reliability of the New York State Transmission System, the NYISO proposes to establish a 
Resource Adequacy Impact Standard as described in Part II.G.ii and a Transmission Security 
Standard as described in Part II.G.iii.  The NYISO and Transmission Owners, as applicable, will 
apply these standards in evaluating the impacts of the proposed interconnection of Large Load 
Projects. 
The NYISO proposes to perform these evaluations for purposes of calculating a Large 
Load Project’s amount of Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service and, if 
requested, its eligibility to be classified as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load.  In making this 
determination, the NYISO will account for available actions to mitigate any adverse reliability 
impacts as described in Part II.G.i.  In particular, a Large Load Project may mitigate its adverse 
impacts to resource adequacy by associating with new supply – an Associated Resource – or by 
taking sufficient Non-Firm Withdrawal Service.  Further, a Large Load Project may mitigate its 
adverse impacts on transmission security by agreeing to proceed with Network Upgrades that 
address the violation or by taking sufficient Non-Firm Withdrawal Service.  For certain 
transmission security violations that can only be resolved with Network Upgrades, the Large 
Load Project will be required to proceed with Network Upgrades.

PDF page 16

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 16 
 
 
f) 
State Exempt Large Load Projects 
After considering comments expressed by the New York State Department of Public 
Service (“NYDPS”) staff during the stakeholder process, the NYISO is proposing tariff language 
under which it would not include Large Load Projects identified by the NYDPS in the standard 
application of the NYISO’s Resource Adequacy Impact Standard.36  Such “State Exempt Large 
Load Projects” would instead be eligible to receive Firm Withdrawal Service so long as their 
collective impact did not cause a resource adequacy violation.37  This topic is discussed further in 
Part II.G.ii below and in the Altman Affidavit.  
 
g) 
Application Process and Readiness Requirements 
The NYISO proposes to establish in Attachment LL detailed application requirements for 
a Large Load Project to participate in and advance through the Large Load Integration Study 
Process.  This includes requirements for the Eligible Customer to submit a Large Load 
Integration Request, a non-refundable Application Fee, a Study Deposit, and additional 
information and requirements as described in Part II.F.ii  The NYISO must review and confirm 
the customer’s satisfaction of these requirements for the Large Load Project to proceed in the 
process.  In addition, the NYISO proposes to establish readiness requirements for Large Load 
Projects.  This includes Site Control requirements that must be satisfied with the Large Load 
Integration Request as described in Part II.F.ii.d and a Readiness Deposit that must be provided 
for the project to enter the Assessment of Load Integration as described in Part II.H.viii.  The 
inclusion of Study Deposits, Site Control requirements, and Readiness Deposits will assist in 
deterring speculative or duplicative submissions as contemplated by the Show Cause Order.38 
h) 
Study Process 
The NYISO proposes to establish a Large Load Study Integration Process in the Large 
Load Integration Procedures that establishes clear and consistent requirements for the evaluation 
of proposed interconnections of Large Load Projects to the New York State Transmission 
System.  The NYISO proposes that this process include an Initial Study Phase as described in 
Part II.H.  This phase includes a Resource Adequacy and Modeling Period and a System Impact 
Study, in which the NYISO and Transmission Owners, as applicable, will perform an initial 
assessment of the proposed interconnection of a Group of Large Load Projects.  This will include 
applying the Resource Adequacy Impact Standard and Transmission Security Standard to assess 
the impacts of the Large Load Projects on the New York State Transmission System and 
evaluating whether the proposed interconnection of each Large Load Project is Physically 
Infeasible.  This study phase will provide Eligible Customers with an initial determination of 
Attachment Facilities and Network Upgrades with preliminary estimates of the costs and 
schedule for constructing these facilities, along with a preliminary determination of each Large

Load Project’s amount of Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service and, if

For Large Load Projects that elect to proceed in the study based on the results of the 
Initial Study Phase, the NYISO proposes to perform an Assessment of Load Integration as 
described in Part II.I.i that will evaluate the remaining projects applying the Resource Adequacy 
Impact Standard and Transmission Security Standard.  The NYISO will identify any violations 
or adverse impacts on the Bulk Electric System and, if more than one project in the Group 
contributes to a violation, then the NYISO will provide the Transmission Owner with the 
projects’ respective contributions to the identified violation.   
In parallel with this analysis, the applicable Transmission Owners will perform Facilities 
Studies for the remaining Large Load Projects as described in Part II.I.ii to: (i) identify any 
violations or adverse impacts on the non-Bulk Electric System and the Large Load Projects’ 
respective contributions to such violations and adverse impacts, (ii) develop Network Upgrades 
to mitigate identified violations, (iii) develop the estimated cost and schedule to construct the 
Network Upgrades, (iv) allocate the cost responsibility of Network Upgrades among Large Load 
Projects in the Group based on their respective contributions to the violation or adverse impact, 
(v) identify the Attachment Facilities required to reliably interconnect the Large Load Projects, 
and (vi) develop the estimated costs and schedule to construct the facilities.  Following the 
performance of these analyses, the NYISO will post on its website a summary of each Large 
Load Project’s required Network Upgrades, including their estimated costs, and each Large Load 
Project’s final amount of Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service and, if 
requested, its eligibility to be classified as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load. 
Finally, as described in Part II.J, the NYISO proposes to establish study requirements: (i) 
to address the impact of Large Load Projects in New York on Affected Systems in New York 
and on neighboring planning regions, and (ii) to address the impacts in New York of load 
projects connecting to neighboring regions. 
i) 
Identification and Cost Allocation of Upgrades 
The NYISO proposes that each Large Load Project participating in the Large Load 
Integration Study Process will be responsible for the costs of the Attachment Facilities and 
Network Upgrades identified for the safe and reliable interconnection of the project, which costs 
will be allocated by the Transmission Owner in the Facilities Study.  As described in Part II.I.ii, 
at the conclusion of the Facilities Study, an Eligible Customer that intends for its project to 
proceed must accept the cost allocation for its Large Load Project’s required Attachment 
Facilities and Network Upgrades and provide the applicable Transmission Owner(s) with 
security to cover its allocated costs.  The Transmission Owners will address in their separate 
Section 205 filing(s) the projects’ cost responsibility for the required facilities as set forth in the 
appropriate agreements (e.g., cost recovery agreement, interconnection agreement, security 
agreement).

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 18 
 
 
j) 
Alternative Transmission Technologies 
As described in Part II.I.ii.b, the NYISO proposes that the Transmission Owner, in 
identifying Network Upgrades in the Facilities Study, consider whether an alternative 
transmission technology could reasonably be implemented to address the identified violation or 
constraint and determine whether the above technologies should be used consistent with Good 
Utility Practice, Applicable Reliability Requirements, and Applicable Laws and Regulations, 
including providing an explanation of the results for each technology.  The alternative 
transmission technologies include static synchronous compensators, static VAR compensators, 
advanced power flow control devices, transmission switching, synchronous condensers, voltage 
source converters, advanced conductors, and tower lifting. 
k) 
Transmission Services Agreement  
As described in Part III.ii, the NYISO proposes to establish requirements for the Eligible 
Customer and the Large Load Project (if the Large Load Project is not its own Eligible Customer 
or Load Serving Entity) to enter into a Transmission Service Agreement with the NYISO for the 
Large Load Project and proposes a pro forma Transmission Service Agreement.39   
l) 
Operational Requirements 
As described in Part III.iii, the NYISO proposes to establish performance, protection and 
control, and operating requirements for Large Load Projects.  Further, the NYISO proposes to 
establish a process as described in Part III.iii by which a project may request an exemption from 
certain of these requirements. 
m) 
Adoption of Requirements from Standard Interconnection 
Procedures 
The NYISO proposes to include in the new Large Load Integration Procedures certain 
process steps and requirements currently used in its Standard Interconnection Procedures for 
generating facilities.  For example, as described in Part II.H.iv, the NYISO proposes to provide 
for the Transmission Owner to assess in the Initial Study Phase whether a project may not 
interconnect due to physical infeasibility, so that such projects can withdraw without incurring or 
causing significant costs, time, and resources.  In addition, the NYISO proposes to use the same 
or similar requirements as those included in its Standard Interconnection Procedures or 
elsewhere in its tariffs for study cost allocation, invoicing and payment requirements, 
confidentiality, dispute resolution, and other standard administrative provisions, which 
requirements have previously been accepted by the Commission as just and reasonable. 
ii. 
Overview of Large Load Integration Procedures  
 
39 Proposed OATT Attach. LL § 44.14.2.

The Large Load Integration Procedures are summarized as follows, which requirements 
are described in detail in Parts II.D through III below, and are illustrated in the overview figure 
provided in Attachment VI. 
The procedures establish that an entity may request at any time information concerning 
the proposed interconnection of a Large Load by requesting a Pre-Application Report as 
described in Part II.D.iii below. 
The procedures further establish the requirements for the recurring Large Load 
Integration Study Process as described in Parts II.E through II.J below.  To enter a particular 
study process cycle, an Eligible Customer may submit a Large Load Integration Request for its 
Large Load Project at any time, including an Application Fee, the Study Deposit 1, information 
regarding any Associated Resources, a demonstration of Site Control, and all other required 
information and materials as described in Part II.F.ii.  The Eligible Customer must timely cure 
any deficiencies identified by the NYISO, Connecting Transmission Owner, or Affected 
Transmission Owner.  If the Eligible Customer submits a valid Large Load Integration Request 
and executes a Large Load Integration Study Agreement, the Large Load Project will be 
included in the next available Group for participation in the study cycle.  
The Large Load Integration Study Process will commence with the Initial Study Phase, 
which will be performed in two successive rounds – Initial Study Round 1 and Initial Study 
Round 2 – as described in Part II.H.ii.  The Initial Study Round 1 will evaluate Large Load 
Projects that satisfied the requirements to enter the study cycle prior to the deadline to enter 
Group 1, and the Initial Study Round 2 will evaluate Large Load Projects that satisfied the 
requirements to enter the study cycle after the deadline to enter Group 1, but prior to the deadline 
to enter Group 2. 
Each round of the Initial Study Phase will include both the Resource Adequacy and 
Modeling Period (“RAMP”) and the System Impact Study (“SIS”).  The RAMP, as described in 
Part II.H.iii will include the following components: (i) the RAMP Part 1 Process, (ii) the SIS 
Entry Decision Period, and (iii) the RAMP Part 2 Process.  For the RAMP Part 1 Process, the 
NYISO will preliminarily develop the base case for the System Impact Study, and the 
Connecting Transmission Owners will prepare preliminary one-line diagrams that identify the 
Attachment Facilities for the Large Load Projects.  The Connecting Transmission Owner will 
also conduct a Physical Infeasibility Screening of the proposed interconnections of the Large 
Load Projects. Finally, the NYISO will perform a Resource Adequacy Evaluation to assess the 
Large Load Projects using the Resource Adequacy Impact Standard.  At the conclusion of the 
RAMP Part 1 Process, the NYISO will commence the SIS Entry Decision Period in which an 
Eligible Customer will elect for its Large Load Project either to proceed to the System Impact 
Study or to withdraw from the Load Queue.  The NYISO will then conduct the RAMP Part 2 
Process to update the base case for the System Impact Study, to develop the scope of the System 
Impact Study, and to conduct a Scoping Meeting with the Eligible Customers with Large Load 
Projects in the Group that elected to proceed to the System Impact Study. 
The NYISO will then perform the System Impact Study in coordination with the 
Connecting Transmission Owners and Affected Transmission Owners as described in Part

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese

II.H.vii.  The System Impact Study will assess the Large Load Projects using the Transmission 
Security Standard.  The Connecting Transmission Owner and Affected Transmission Owner will 
preliminarily determine the Attachment Facilities and the Network Upgrades required for the 
Large Load Project, along with non-binding cost estimates for such facilities, including such 
facilities necessary for the Large Load Project to be eligible as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load, 
if requested.  The NYISO will preliminarily determine the amount of Firm Withdrawal Service 
or Non-Firm Withdrawal Service for the Large Load Projects and, if requested, whether the 
Large Load Project is eligible to be a Zero Withdrawal Service Load.  The System Impact Study 
results will be subject to stakeholder review and comment and must be approved by the 
stakeholder Operating Committee.  Following the completion of the draft System Impact Study 
Report for Group 1 for the Initial Study Round 1, the NYISO will commence the Initial Study 
Round 2 for Group 2, which will be performed in accordance with the same requirements as for 
Group 1. 
After the completion of the Initial Study Round 2 for Group 2, the NYISO will 
commence the ALI Entry Decision Period as described in Part II.H.viii.  For this decision period, 
an Eligible Customer for a Large Load Project that participated in either the Initial Study Round 
1 or the Initial Study Round 2 of the Initial Study Phase will either: (i) elect for its Large Load 
Project to proceed to the Assessment of Load Integration and submit to the NYISO its Study 
Deposit 2 and Readiness Deposit or (ii) withdraw its Large Load Project from the Load Queue. 
The NYISO will then commence the Assessment of Load Integration (“ALI”) as 
described in Part II.I.i for the Group of Large Load Projects that elected to proceed, while the 
Connecting Transmission Owners and any Affected Transmission Owners will commence in 
parallel the Facilities Studies for the Large Load Project(s) as described in Part II.I.ii.  For the 
ALI Part 1 Study, the NYISO will (i) evaluate the impact on the Bulk Electric System of the 
proposed interconnection of the Large Load Projects using the Resource Adequacy Impact 
Standard and the Transmission Security Standard, (ii) identify any violations or adverse impacts 
caused by one or more of the Large Load Projects, (iii) if more than one project contributes to a 
violation or adverse impact, identify and provide to the Transmission Owners the projects’ 
respective contributions to the identified violation, and (iv) update the amounts of Firm 
Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service for each Large Load Project.  During the 
Facilities Study, each Connecting Transmission Owner and Affected Transmission Owner will 
determine and cost allocate the Attachment Facilities and Local Network Upgrades and Non-
Local Network Upgrades to mitigate violations identified by the Connecting Transmission 
Owner or Affected Transmission Owner on the non-Bulk Electric System, including facilities 
necessary for the Large Load Project to be eligible as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load, if 
requested.  Where more than one Large Load Project contributes to a violation on the non-Bulk 
Electric System, the Transmission Owner will determine the projects’ respective contributions.   
Following the ALI Part 1 Study and the NYISO’s identification of any violations on the 
Bulk Electric System and projects’ respective contributions to the violations, each Connecting 
Transmission Owner and Affected Transmission Owner will determine the Non-Local Network 
Upgrades to mitigate violations identified by the NYISO on the Bulk Electric System and the 
cost allocation of each shared upgrade based on the projects’ respective contribution to the 
violations provided by the NYISO for the Bulk Electric System or identified to the Transmission

Owner for the non-Bulk Electric System.  The NYISO will then confirm in the ALI Part 2 Study 
the Non-Local Network Upgrades identified by the Transmission Owner, will designate the final 
amounts of Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service for each Large Load 
Project, and will confirm eligibility to be classified as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load, if 
requested.  To proceed with its Large Load Project, the Eligible Customer must accept its cost

Following the completion of the Large Load Integration Study Process cycle, the Eligible

described in Part III.i.  In addition, the Eligible Customer must enter into a Transmission Service 
Agreement with the NYISO and the Large Load (if the Large Load is not its own Transmission 
Customer or Load Serving Entity) concerning its amounts of Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-
Firm Withdrawal Service as described in Part III.ii. 
D. 
Scope and Effective Date of Large Load Integration Procedures 
i. 
Application of Large Load Integration Procedures  
The NYISO proposes that the Large Load Integration Procedures apply: (i) to proposed 
interconnections of a Large Load Project to the New York State Transmission System and (ii) to 
proposals to make a Material Modification to an existing load or a Large Load Project 
interconnected or seeking to interconnect to the New York State Transmission System.40  The 
NYISO proposes to define a Large Load Project consistent with its definition of Large Load, as 
described in Part II.A.41   
 
The NYISO proposes to carve out of the Large Load Integration Procedures load projects 
that are public safety facilities and providers of essential services (e.g., emergency medical 
services, first responder facilities, or public water supply and treatment facilities), station service, 
and Critical Electric System Infrastructure Loads.42  These facilities provide essential public-
safety or basic public-health services for which delays or service limitations could create 
significant public welfare consequences.  These projects will still be subject to the applicable 
Transmission Owner’s load interconnection procedures, including any requirements concerning 
safe and reliable service.  In addition, the NYISO proposes that the procedures not apply to a

requirements for modifications are described in Part II.F.vi. 
41 Proposed OATT Attach. LL §§ 44.1 (definition of Large Load Project), 44.3.1.1.  Large Load Projects 
include Computational Large Loads and Non-Computational Large Loads. 
42 Proposed OATT Attach. LL § 44.3.1.2.

commercial or industrial Load proposing to connect or connecting to the Long Island 
Transmission District system at a voltage of 69 kV and below.43  This exclusion accounts for the 
Long Island Power Authority’s responsibilities concerning the Long Island Transmission District 
and is consistent with the Commission’s proposal that large loads include load interconnecting to 
the transmission system at a voltage level of greater than 69 kV. 
A load project that is not subject to the Large Load Integration Procedures will be subject 
to the load interconnection procedures of the Transmission Owner to which system it is seeking 
to interconnect.44  In addition, the new procedures will not apply to the evaluation of the 
interconnection of Associated Resources.45  Such resources will instead be assessed in the 
applicable generation interconnection procedures or, for non-generation projects, the applicable 
requirements for registering the resource. 
ii. 
Effective Date of Large Load Integration Procedures and Transition 
Requirements 
In the Show Cause Order, the Commission recognized that different large loads are 
currently at different stages of existing interconnection processes and that large loads and 
Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of large loads are negotiating, entering 
into, and/or have executed various agreements related to interconnecting to the transmission 
system and/or the provision of transmission service.46  Accordingly, the Commission indicated 
that any tariff revisions should “(a) propose a reasonable implementation period to ensure 
minimal disruption to such existing commercial arrangements, and (b) allow a reasonable 
amount of time to finalize ongoing agreements that are nearing completion as of the date such 
Tariff provisions are filed with the Commission.”47  Further, the Commission indicated that 
“[s]uch proposed Tariff revisions should include a reasonable effective date that accommodates 
such considerations regarding these existing commercial arrangements.”48 
The NYISO proposes that the Large Load Integration Procedures become effective on 
February 1, 2027 as described in Part IX.49  Upon this effective date, the NYISO’s existing load 
interconnection rules in Sections 3.9 and 4.5 of the NYISO OATT will no longer apply and the 
existing load projects in the NYISO’s Queue will be withdrawn, except as otherwise provided in 
the transition rules described below.50   
As described below and in the Nguyen Affidavit, the NYISO proposes to establish 
transition rules for existing load projects in its Queue that reasonably balance the timely 
implementation of the new requirements adopted in response to Show Cause Order with

minimizing disruptions to existing projects that have already completed or progressed to certain 
defined milestones in the NYISO’s existing load interconnection process.51 
First, the NYISO proposes to retain the Queue Position of a load project in its Queue that, 
as of the effective date of the new procedures, has completed the study process required by 
Section 3.9 of the OATT, but has not yet entered service.52  These load projects have or are in the 
process of entering into the applicable interconnection agreements and commercial 
arrangements.    
Second, the NYISO proposes to establish a transition group – Transition Group A – for 
load projects that currently have a Queue Position, satisfy the definition of a Large Load Project, 
and have met certain milestones and requirements that demonstrate substantial progress in the 
existing interconnection process in Section 3.9 of the OATT.53  These projects have already 
progressed to at least the facilities study stage of the existing process and have provided evidence 
of their readiness to proceed.  Moreover, as described in the Affidavit of James Ross Altman, 
Senior Manager, Reliability Planning for the NYISO (“Altman Affidavit”), the resource 
adequacy impacts for these projects have already been studied in the NYISO’s biennial 
reliability assessment, which is pending approval with the NYISO’s Board of Directors, and such 
impacts will be managed through the Reliability Planning Process.54  Accordingly, the NYISO 
proposes that these projects may elect to complete the facilities study and proceed in accordance 
with the existing requirements in Section 3.9 of the OATT.55 
In particular, to proceed in Transition Group A, an existing load project that satisfies the 
definition of a Large Load Project must satisfy or have satisfied all of the following 
requirements: (i) has submitted an interconnection request before June 18, 2026, (ii) has received 
Operating Committee approval of the scope of its system impact study before June 18, 2026, (iii) 
has received Operating Committee approval of the report of its system impact study before 
March 1, 2027, (iv) has one or more qualifying agreements before November 16, 2026,56 (v) has 
an ongoing or completed facilities study with a Connecting Transmission Owner and any 
Affected Transmission Owner before November 16, 2026, or a facilities study is not required for 
its load project, and (vi) has posted security or made a milestone payment to the Connecting 
Transmission Owner and any Affected Transmission Owner by March 1, 2027.57  A load project 
that elects to proceed in Transition Group A to complete its evaluation in accordance with the

existing requirements in Section 3.9 of the OATT will be responsible for mitigating any 
transmission security violations.58  That is, such load project that is assessed under the existing 
tariff rules will be eligible for transmission service consistent with the NYISO’s existing tariff 
requirements and will not be eligible to obtain Non-Firm Withdrawal Service or to be classified 
as a Zero Withdrawal Service Load.  The project may elect to instead enter Transition Group B if 
it wants to obtain such alternative Withdrawal Service under the new tariff rules. 
Third, the NYISO proposes to establish a transition group – Transition Group B – for 
load projects that currently have a Queue Position, satisfy the definition of a Large Load Project, 
and have met certain milestones in the existing interconnection process in Section 3.9 of the 
OATT, but are not as far advanced in the existing process as projects eligible to participate in 
Transition Group A.59  These projects have already progressed to at least the conclusion of their 
system impact study in the existing tariff process and have provided evidence of their readiness 
to proceed.  The Transition Group B projects would be eligible to participate in the first Large 
Load Integration Study Process – i.e., the Transition Large Load Integration Study Process.60   
In particular, to proceed in Transition Group B, an existing load project that satisfies the 
definition of a Large Load Project must satisfy or have satisfied all of the following 
requirements: (i) has submitted an interconnection request before June 18, 2026, (ii) has received 
Operating Committee approval of the scope of its system impact study before November 16, 
2026, (iii) has received Operating Committee approval of the report of its system impact study 
before June 1, 2027, (iv) has one or more fully executed qualifying agreements before June 1, 
2027,61 and (v) does not satisfy the requirements to enter Transition Group A or has satisfied 
these requirement but provides notice to the NYISO of its election to enter Transition Group B 
instead to be eligible to obtain Non-Firm Withdrawal Service or, if requested, be eligible to be 
classified as Zero Withdrawal Service Load.62   
A load project that elects to proceed in Transition Group B may participate in the 
Transition Large Load Integration Study Process.  To do so, it must submit a Large Load 
Integration Request as part of the transition process and will receive a new Load Queue Position.  
The transition process will be largely consistent with the NYISO’s proposed standard Large 
Load Integration Study Process, except that the transition process will not include a System 
Impact Study (or the related requirements for developing such study) as the Transition Group B 
projects would have already been subject to a system impact study under the Section 3.9 
requirements.63

Finally, the NYISO proposes that it will proceed to complete a system impact study

an interconnection request for a load project that is smaller than 50 MW and (ii) the NYISO has

in the Queue for the duration of the system impact study and would withdraw the project from 
the Queue upon the delivery of the study report to the Eligible Customer or termination of the 
study.  As the projects are smaller than 50 MW, they will not be eligible to proceed in the 
NYISO’s new process, but the Eligible Customer may complete the system impact study to

transition requirements, the NYISO will withdraw it from the Queue following the effective date 
of the new Large Load Integration Procedures.65  If the withdrawn project satisfies the 
requirements to participate in the new procedures, the Eligible Customer may reapply to 
participate in the new Large Load Integration Study Process by submitting a Large Load 
Integration Request in accordance with the rules described below.66  If the withdrawn project 
does not satisfy the new requirements for participation in the Large Load Integration Study 
Process, it must be evaluated in accordance with the applicable Transmission Owner’s load 
interconnection procedures.67 
iii. 
Pre-Application Reports 
The NYISO proposes to establish a process in the Large Load Integration Procedures by 
which an entity may request a Pre-Application Report for information regarding the proposed 
interconnection of a load project consistent with the definition of Large Load at a particular point 
on the New York State Transmission System.68  The proposed process is based on and consistent 
with the Pre-Application Report process in the NYISO’s Standard Interconnection Procedures in 
Attachment HH of the OATT.69  It creates a mechanism by which a prospective Eligible 
Customer may obtain information based on readily available data concerning potential Points of 
Interconnection to enhance its ability to develop a Large Load Integration Request for a Large 
Load Project.  Based on the NYISO’s experience in its Standard Interconnection Procedures as

described in the Nguyen Affidavit, the Pre-Application Report process has improved the

as completed using readily available data to the extent available.75  This pre-application process 
is not a mandatory step for an Eligible Customer to proceed into the Large Load Integration 
Process and is solely for information purposes and is non-binding.76  The Eligible Customer will 
be informed if the proposed interconnection is not subject to the Large Load Integration 
Procedures, and the information report will be completed to the extent possible.77

2027.79  The NYISO will commence each subsequent Large Load Integration Study Process on 
the latter of: (i) one year after the start date of the previous Large Load Integration Study 
Process, or (ii) the first Calendar Day of the next month following the completion of the most 
recent ongoing or completed Assessment of Load Integration and Facilities Study.80  The 
proposed mechanism for establishing the start date will allow for an end-to-end process whereby 
the Assessment of Load Integration must be completed for the Group of Large Load Projects 
participating in each study cycle, including the determination of their cost allocation and 
provision of security, prior to the commencement of the next Assessment of Load Integration.  
This will ensure greater certainty concerning process results and reduce potential issues that can 
arise from the overlapping study work. 
ii. 
Large Load Integration Study Process Timeframe and Process Overlap 
The NYISO proposes a timeframe of between approximately one year and six months 
and two years for the performance of the Large Load Integration Study Process cycle, which 
includes a 90-day time period to perform the System Impact Study.  The overall process 
timeframe for a particular Large Load Project will depend on when the Eligible Customer has 
satisfied the application requirements for its project to enter the study cycle.  The time period 
includes the two rounds of the Initial Study Phase for the study cycle, which require 
approximately six months each, and then the performance of the Assessment of Load Integration 
and Facilities Study, which are performed in parallel and require approximately one year.81  The 
proposed timeframe is illustrated in the figure provided in Attachment VII. 
The NYISO acknowledges that the proposed timeframe is longer than the process 
duration contemplated by the Show Cause Order.82  However, the timeframe constitutes a just 
and reasonable period of time in light of the scope of the Large Load Integration Study Process 
and ensures that the NYISO, Transmission Owners, and Eligible Customers are able to perform 
their respective responsibilities under this process.  As detailed in the Nguyen Affidavit, the 
 
79 Id. §§ 44.1 (definition of Large Load Integration Study Process Start Date), 44.6.1.2.  The duration of the 
Initial Study Phase for the Transition Large Load Integration Study Process will be shorter than for a subsequent 
process because there will not be a System Impact Study for the transition process.  Accordingly, the NYISO is able 
to commence the first regular Large Load Integration Study Process a month following the start of the transition 
process.  The NYISO will complete the Assessment of Load Integration for the Transition Large Load Integration 
Study Process prior to the commencement of the Assessment of Load Integration for the subsequent Large Load 
Integration Study Process.

82 The Show Cause Order proposes a process duration of 60 to 90 calendar days, resulting in a study that 
includes any Direct Assignment Facilities and Network Upgrades necessary to accommodate the transmission

Show Cause Order at P 69.  However, the Commission did not detail in the Show Cause Order the scope of the 
study work that permitted that duration.  The Commission did reference Southwest Power Pool’s (“SPP”) 90-day 
timeframe for its High Impact Large Load (“HILL”) process as an example of the 90-day duration.  However, the 
SPP study is an individual study with non-binding results.  SPP has noted that cluster studies may be needed if the 
volume of large loads becomes large and that such cluster studies will take longer than 90 days, including to address 
the impacts of customers withdrawing.  Comments of Southwest Power Pool, Inc., Interconnection of Large Loads to

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 28 
 
 
NYISO developed the timeframes for the different study elements, in coordination with the 
Transmission Owners, based on their extensive experience in performing similar administrative 
and study work in the existing load interconnection procedures, their performance of comparable 
study work and process steps in the NYISO’s Standard Interconnection Procedures for generator 
interconnections, and reasonable estimates of the time required to address new process elements 
required to account for the analyses contemplated by the Show Cause Order and process 
enhancements detailed in this filing.83  
The timeframes also account for sufficient time for Eligible Customers to participate in a 
scoping meeting, to review and comment on study results in the different study phases, and to 
make informed business decisions on whether to proceed with their projects to subsequent study 
phases.  In addition, this timeframe will provide opportunities for stakeholder review and input in 
the process.  Finally, while the grouping of Large Load Projects into consolidated studies will 
provide for a more efficient overall process that improves the identification of upgrades across 
the interconnecting projects than through the performance of overlapping individual studies, 
assessing projects on a group basis increases the complexity in preparing the studies and the 
technical analyses with a resulting increase on the time required to perform the studies.  This 
includes the time required to account for the impacts of projects withdrawing from a Group 
during the study process or at one of the decision points. 
The NYISO also proposes that the performance of a given Large Load Integration Study 
Process overlap with the performance of elements of the prior Transition Large Load Integration 
Study Process or Large Load Integration Study Process.84  In particular, the NYISO proposes to 
perform the two rounds of the Initial Study Phase of a given Large Load Integration Study 
Process cycle in parallel with the performance of the Assessment of Load Integration and 
Facilities Study stages of the prior study cycle.  This will enable the NYISO to commence the 
assessment of additional load projects approximately every six months while completing the 
study work for the prior study cycle. 
The NYISO proposes to establish in Attachment LL certain tariff-prescribed timeframes 
for elements of the Large Load Integration Study Process as described throughout this filing.  If 
the NYISO, Connecting Transmission Owner, Affected Transmission Owner, or Affected 
System Operator is unable to complete an individual component of the process in accordance 
with that component’s timeframe established in Attachment LL, the NYISO proposes that the 
entity responsible for performing that component will complete it as soon as practicable, and the 
NYISO will notify Eligible Customers of any anticipated resulting delay in the overall timeframe 
of the study process.85

elements in the existing load interconnection process in the NYISO’s OATT.  In particular, the 
Commission indicated the absence of: “(i) an application process that accepts Eligible

i. 
Application Process and Timeframe Requirements for Submission of Large 
Load Integration Request 
The NYISO proposes to require that an Eligible Customer submit a Large Load 
Integration Request and satisfy additional entry requirements for a Large Load Project to enter a 
Large Load Integration Study Process.87  For purposes of the Transition Large Load Integration 
Study Process, the NYISO proposes to establish an application window that commences on 
March 1, 2027, and concludes on May 31, 2027, during which a project in Transition Group B 
may satisfy the requirements to enter the study process.88  The NYISO proposes to separately 
begin accepting Large Load Integration Requests on April 1, 2027, for Large Load Projects that 
intend to enter the first regular Large Load Integration Study Process or subsequent processes.89  
Beginning on that date, an Eligible Customer may submit a Large Load Integration Request at 
any time.90   
Starting with its first Large Load Integration Study Process after the transition process, 
the NYISO proposes to include a Large Load Project with a validated Large Load Integration 
Request and an executed Large Load Integration Study Agreement into the next available Group 
of projects (i.e., Group 1 or Group 2) that will be evaluated in a Large Load Integration Study 
Process.91  In particular, the NYISO will include a Large Load Project in Group 1 for the Initial 
Study Round 1 of a given study process if the project has not yet been included in a Group 
evaluated in a previous Large Load Integration Study Process cycle and has satisfied the 
requirements to enter a Group prior to the deadline for entering Group 1.92  The NYISO will 
include a Large Load Project in Group 2 for the Initial Study Round 2 if the project has satisfied

ii. 
Submission Requirements for Large Load Integration Requests  
The NYISO proposes that an Eligible Customer be required to submit the following 
information and data, fees, and deposits for purposes of entering the Large Load Integration 
Study Process. 
a) 
Request Form 
The Eligible Customer must submit a completed Large Load Integration Request in 
accordance with the form established in Appendix 1 to Attachment LL.94  The standard form 
provides for the Eligible Customer to submit information required for the evaluation of the Large 
Load Project in the Large Load Integration Study Process, including information regarding the 
project’s size, its proposed Point of Interconnection, its association with any Associated 
Resources, the expected nature of the load project, modeling and operational data, and evidence 
of its satisfaction of the additional project entry requirements (e.g., Application Fee, Study 
Deposit, Site Control).95  In addition, consistent with the requirements in the NYISO’s 
Interconnection Request form for its Standard Interconnection Procedures,96 the Large Load 
Integration Request includes an attestation form affirming the accuracy of the submitted 
information and Eligible Customer’s responsibility for study costs, including costs incurred prior 
to the full execution of the Large Load Integration Study Agreement. 
The Show Cause Order identified the lack of requirements in the NYISO’s existing tariffs 
for an Eligible Customer’s disclosure to the NYISO of any substantially similar pending 
transmission service requests on behalf of the same large load customer.97  The NYISO proposes 
to clarify as part of the Large Load Integration Request requirements that an Eligible Customer, 
or an Eligible Customer and one of its Affiliates, cannot submit a request for a mutually 
exclusive Large Load Project with a Large Load Project in the Load Queue or with a project for 
which a Large Load Integration Request has been submitted and the NYISO is evaluating for 
purposes of validation.98  This is necessary to deter speculative Large Load Integration Requests, 
including Eligible Customers proposing multiple variations of proposed interconnections.99  In 
addition, this is required to enable the identification of physically infeasible projects.  The 
NYISO’s proposed approach is consistent with its restriction of the submission of mutually 
exclusive Interconnection Requests in its Standard Interconnection Procedures.

provide a study deposit of $150,000.104  While this reasonably covers the costs of the study work

increased study costs going forward due to more complex studies resulting from more MWs

d) 
Readiness Requirements/ Site Control 
In the Show Cause Order, the Commission indicated that clear and consistent study 
procedures could include readiness requirements (i.e. meaningful milestones and/or financial 
commitments) to help ensure that Eligible Customers’ requests for transmission service on behalf 
of large loads are more viable and more likely to materialize as planned, given that they must 
meet specific milestones and/or pay costs to proceed.”107   
The NYISO proposes two mechanisms for the Eligible Customer to demonstrate the 
readiness of its project – (i) a demonstration of full Site Control for the Large Load with the 
Large Load Integration Request108 and (ii) a Readiness Deposit during the ALI Entry Decision 
Period, which is described in Part II.H.viii.  As described in the Nguyen Affidavit, an Eligible 
Customer’s satisfaction of these requirements provides reasonable evidence of meaningful 
progress and financial commitment in the development of the Large Load Project.109 
For purposes of the Site Control requirements, the NYISO proposes to define Site 
Control consistent with its definition in its Standard Interconnection Procedures adopted in 
response to Order No. 2023.110  To satisfy the Site Control requirement, the Eligible Customer

A Large Load Integration Request will not be considered to be a valid request until the 
NYISO has confirmed all of the tariff required items.118  Within 5 Business Days of its receipt of 
a Large Load Integration Request submission that includes all of the required items, the NYISO 
will (i) acknowledge receipt of the request, (ii) confirm receipt of the Application Fee and Study 
Deposit 1, (iii) identify the Connecting Transmission Owner(s) with which the Large Load is 
proposing to connect and any Affected Transmission Owner(s) and Affected Systems that the 
NYISO is aware of, and (iv) make available the information submitted with the Large Load 
Integration Request and its acknowledgement to the Connecting Transmission Owner(s) and any 
identified Affected Transmission Owner(s) and Affected Systems.119  Within this same time 
period, the Connecting Transmission Owner identified by the NYISO must confirm it is the 
appropriate Connecting Transmission Owner and confirm the appropriate Affected Transmission 
Owner(s) and Affected Systems.120 
Within 15 Business Days of the conclusion of the initial 5 Business Day period, the 
NYISO will confirm whether all of the elements of the Large Load Integration Request comply 
with the tariff requirements and review the provided modeling data and models.121  Within this 
period, the NYISO will notify the Eligible Customer whether its Large Load Integration Request 
is valid or includes any deficiencies.122  The Eligible Customer must provide the NYISO with 
information required to address a deficiency within 10 Business Days after receipt of such 
notice.123  Within 10 Business Days of this submission, the NYISO will review and, if it 
determines the identified deficiency has not been addressed, will notify the Eligible Customer of 
the remaining deficiency, which the Eligible Customer must address pursuant to the same 
process as the initially identified deficiencies.124  If the NYISO determines that Eligible 
Customer’s Large Load Integration Request is valid or that the Eligible Customer has addressed 
any deficiencies within the required timeframe, the NYISO will notify the Eligible Customer that 
the Large Load Integration Request is valid, and such request will proceed as part of the Load 
Queue.125   
The NYISO will maintain on its OASIS or publicly accessible portion of its website a list 
of all valid Large Load Integration Requests, including whether a Large Load Project is a State 
Exempt Large Load Project.126  If the Eligible Customer fails to submit additional information

iv. 
Study Agreement 
The NYISO proposes to require the Eligible Customer to enter into a study agreement – 
the Large Load Integration Study Agreement – for the Large Load Integration Study Process 
with the NYISO, the Connecting Transmission Owner, and any identified Affected Transmission 
Owner(s) or Affected System Owner(s).130  The NYISO proposes to establish a pro forma study 
agreement form in Appendix 6 to Attachment LL.  The form agreement is based on the study 
agreement used for the Cluster Study Process in the NYISO’s Standard Interconnection 
Procedures as modified to address the specific terms and requirements for the Large Load 
Integration Study Process. 
The NYISO proposes to tender an executable version of the study agreement as soon as 
practicable after its verification that the Eligible Customer’s Large Load Integration Request is 
valid.131  Within 10 Calendar Days of the tendering of the agreement, the Eligible Customer, 
Connecting Transmission Owner(s), and any Affected Transmission Owner(s) or Affected 
System Operator(s) must execute the study agreement.132

The NYISO proposes to establish a Load Queue for projects participating in the Large 
Load Integration Study Process.133  The NYISO will assign a Load Queue Position for a Large 
Load Integration Request based upon the date and time of the NYISO’s receipt of the Eligible 
Customer’s complete submission of the request.134  If the NYISO validates the Large Load 
Integration Request, then the request will retain its assigned Load Queue Position based on the 
date and time the submission was originally filed.135  This is consistent with the NYISO’s Queue 
requirements in its Standard Interconnection Procedures.136 
All Large Load Integration Requests that are a part of a single Group for the Large Load 
Integration Study Process will be considered equally queued as between the Large Load 
Integration Requests within the same Group.137  However, a Large Load Integration Request’s 
individual Load Queue Position will be used to determine priority as between Large Load 
Integration Requests in the same Group in the event of a Physical Infeasibility determination as 
described in Part II.H.iv.a.138  Large Load Integration Requests that are part of a particular Large 
Load Integration Study Process will be considered to have a higher Load Queue Position than 
requests that are part of a subsequent study process.139  
vi. 
Modifications 
The NYISO proposes to establish requirements in the Large Load Integration Procedures 
for an Eligible Customer to request that the NYISO evaluate whether the Eligible Customer’s 
proposed modification to its existing load interconnected to or proposing to interconnect to the 
New York State Transmission System or to its Large Load Project is permitted or is a Material 
Modification.140  If the NYISO determines pursuant to this process that a proposed modification 
is a Material Modification, the Eligible Customer may either withdraw the proposed 
modification request or submit a new Large Load Integration Request in a subsequent Large 
Load Integration Study Process to be studied for such modification.141

The NYISO proposes modification procedures based on the modification requirements in 
its Standard Interconnection Procedures.  To request a modification, an Eligible Customer must 
submit to the NYISO: (i) a Load Modification Request in the form of Appendix 3 to this

the Facilities Study in which the Large Load Project is participating, which modification would 
constitute a Material Modification.149  As described in the Nguyen Affidavit, this is because such 
modifications would require updated models which may require updates to base cases and 
auxiliary study files and therefore would delay the performance of the study adversely impacting 
all study participants.150  Once the study is completed, an Eligible Customer may again request

the NYISO’s review of proposed modifications to determine whether they are permitted under
