B. 
Additional Requirements 
i. 
Study Cost Allocation, Invoicing, and Deposit Requirements  
The NYISO proposes to establish tariff requirements that describe the Eligible 
Customer’s cost responsibilities for the study work in the Large Load Integration Procedures, the 
invoicing and payment requirements for these study costs, and the rules for the use and refund of 
deposits provided under these procedures.434  These requirements are based on the similar 
requirements in the NYISO’s Standard Interconnection Procedures.435 
a) 
Study Cost Responsibility and Allocation of Study Costs 
The NYISO proposes that it will charge, and the Eligible Customer will pay, the actual 
costs of the study work incurred by the NYISO and Transmission Owner for the Large Load 
Integration Study Process, after the Eligible Customer has submitted its Large Load Integration 
Request.436  The NYISO proposes to allocate the costs of the Large Load Integration Study 
Process among the Eligible Customers for their projects participating in the process so that the 
Eligible Customer is only responsible for the costs associated with the study of its particular 
project.  In particular, each Eligible Customer must pay an equal share of the Large Load 
Integration Study Process costs except for the study of Attachment Facilities, which are specific 
to each project.437  For Attachment Facilities, the Eligible Customer must pay the actual costs of 
studying the Attachment Facilities for its own project(s).438  If more than one Large Load Project 
contributes to the need for particular Attachment Facilities, those Large Load Projects will share 
equally in the cost to study those Attachment Facilities.439 
For an Affected System Load Study, the NYISO proposes that it will charge, and the 
Affected System Eligible Customer will pay, the actual costs of the study work of that study.440  
The NYISO proposes to allocate such costs among Affected System Eligible Customers 
participating in the Affected System Load Study, so that each project participating in the study 
must pay an equal share of the study costs required for the identification of the need for any 
Affected System Network Upgrades.441  If more than one project contributes to the need for

Finally, for requested modifications to load facilities and projects, the NYISO proposes 
that it will charge, and the entity that submitted a Load Modification Request will pay, the actual 
costs of the NYISO’s review and any required studies for the Load Modification Request. 
The NYISO’s proposed approach allocates study costs in accordance with cost causation 
principles to the particular entities responsible for such costs in place of a more general 
allocation of costs. 
b) 
Invoicing and Payment Requirements  
The NYISO proposes to invoice an Eligible Customer on a monthly basis for study 
costs.443  A monthly invoicing approach is consistent with how the NYISO invoices customers in 
its other interconnection process and provides transparency to customers of their study costs 
throughout the study process and timely reimbursement of costs incurred by the NYISO. 
The Eligible Customer must pay the invoiced amount to the NYISO within thirty 30 
Calendar Days of the NYISO’s issuance of the invoice.444  Except as otherwise provided in the 
billing dispute rules, if the Eligible Customer does not pay its invoice within this period, it will 
be subject to withdrawal and will be assessed interest on any unpaid invoiced amounts calculated 
in accordance with section 35.19a(a)(2) of FERC’s regulations.445  To dispute an invoiced 
amount, an Eligible Customer must (i) timely pay any undisputed amounts to the NYISO and (ii) 
pay into an independent escrow account the portion of the invoice in dispute, pending resolution 
of such dispute.446  If the Eligible Customer fails to meet these two requirements, then neither the 
NYISO nor Transmission Owner is obligated to perform or continue to perform any study work 
on behalf of the Eligible Customer’s Large Load Integration Request, Load Modification 
Request, or Affected System Load Study.447  A billing dispute will be addressed through the 
dispute resolution procedures set forth for the Large Load Integration Procedures as described in 
Part III.B.i.b.448  Within thirty 30 Calendar Days after resolution of the dispute, the Eligible 
Customer will pay the NYISO any amounts due with interest actually earned on such amounts.449 
Any invoices for the Transition Large Load Integration Study Process, the Large Load 
Integration Study Process, any study resulting from a Load Modification Request, or an Affected 
System Load Study must be submitted to the NYISO within 60 days of completion of the subject 
study and must include a detailed and itemized accounting of the incurred cost of the applicable

study work.450  After the conclusion of the applicable study or if, prior to the conclusion of the

c) 
Requirements Applicable to Fees and Deposits 
The NYISO proposes to permit entities to provide their required deposits (e.g., Study 
Deposits, Readiness Deposits) for the Large Load Integration Study Process in the form of cash, 
a letter of credit, or a surety bond, while they must satisfy any fees using cash (e.g., the 
application fee).452  
The NYISO proposes to clarify in Attachment LL its requirements for accepting cash, 
letters of credit, or surety bonds for deposits.453  These requirements are consistent with the 
NYISO’s existing credit requirements under its tariffs applicable to Market Participants and its 
requirements for fees and deposits in its Standard Interconnection Procedures.454 
The NYISO also proposes to specify how it will hold and apply such deposits, refund any 
cash deposits, and/or provide authorization to the entity that provided the deposit to request that 
the issuing entity cancel a letter of credit or surety bond.455  In particular, the NYISO will hold 
deposits provided by an Eligible Customer until settlement of the final invoices.  If an Eligible 
Customer has not paid all invoices, the NYISO will recover any unpaid costs from Eligible 
Customer’s deposits. After the NYISO has recovered all unpaid costs, if any, from Eligible 
Customer’s deposits, the NYISO will (i) refund to the Eligible Customer any remaining 
refundable cash portion of its deposits, and (ii) provide written authorization for Eligible 
Customer to request that the bank cancel any remaining letter of credit or surety bond provided 
as a deposit. 
These invoicing, payment, and deposit rules do not apply to the Facilities Study or to the 
requirements under the Facilities Study agreement, security agreement, cost recovery agreement, 
or interconnection agreement that the Transmission Owners will submit in their separate Section 
205 filing(s).

The NYISO proposes to include in its Large Load Integration Procedures requirements 
concerning the treatment of Confidential Information exchanged by the parties during the 
administration of the procedures.456  In particular, the NYISO proposes to use the same

iii. 
Dispute Resolution Requirements  
The NYISO proposes to establish that a party may address a dispute that arises out of or 
in connection with the Large Load Integration Procedures or their performance in accordance 
with its standard dispute resolution procedures set forth in Article 11 of the NYISO’s Services 
Tariff.458  Pursuant to these procedures, parties first attempt to resolve a dispute through informal 
discussions.459  If the dispute is not resolved, the parties may agree to proceed with non-binding 
mediation or arbitration as further detailed in Article 11, or may commence legal proceedings 
before the Commission or a court of competent jurisdiction.460 
iv. 
Third Party Conducting Studies 
The NYISO proposes to establish requirements for the use of third parties to conduct 
study work under the Large Load Integration Procedures that is generally consistent with the 
similar requirements for its Standard Interconnection Procedures.461  In particular, the NYISO 
proposes that the NYISO, a Transmission Owner, or an Affected System Operator may use a 
Transmission Owner or other third party to perform its respective obligations under the Large 
Load Integration Procedures.462  The NYISO proposes that the use of a third party be limited to 
situations where the NYISO determines that doing so will help maintain or accelerate the study 
process.463  Such third-party consultants must comply with the relevant NYISO OATT, 
procedures, and protocols as would apply if the NYISO were to conduct the study process and 
must use the information provided to it solely for purposes of performing such services and for 
no other purposes.464 
v. 
Additional Requirements  
The NYISO proposes to include in the Large Load Integration Procedures certain process 
requirements concerning data requirements, limitations of liability, and reserved rights that are 
based on related requirements in its Standard Interconnection Procedures in Attachment HH of 
the OATT, which requirements the Commission has previously accepted as just and reasonable.

First, the NYISO proposes to establish that Eligible Customers must provide the NYISO

in accordance with the NYISO’s OATT, Commission-approved ISO Related Agreements, the 
Federal Power Act and Commission precedent, and, therefore, will be subject to the Connecting 
Transmission Owner’s or Affected Transmission Owner’s right to recover, pursuant to 
appropriate financial arrangements contained in agreements or Commission-approved tariffs, all 
reasonably incurred costs, plus a reasonable return on investment.469

i. 
Firm Withdrawal Service Loads 
Large Loads that are eligible to receive Firm Withdrawal Service for their full amount of 
Load are fully represented in the Load forecasts that inform the IRM, LCRs, and ultimately 
ICAP auction outcomes, and submit traditional Load bids according to existing Load bidding 
rules, may register as Demand Side Resources (with limitations), and are not eligible to 
participate in the NYISO’s proposed CLLC model. 
As discussed in the attached affidavit of Zachary T. Smith, the NYISO’s Director of 
Market Solutions (“Smith Affidavit”), it is reasonable for Large Loads or the portions thereof 
that are receiving Firm Withdrawal Service to be included in the load forecast for the IRM and 
LCRs.492  This inclusion is the foundation of the NYISO’s obligation to provide Firm 
Withdrawal Service continuously and regardless of price, consistent with the way that firm 
transmission service is provided to existing load resources in the NYISO today.  
It is also reasonable for Full Withdrawal Service Large Loads to engage with the markets 
using the NYISO’s established Load bidding rules.  The Large Load’s LSE may submit Fixed or 
Price Capped Load bids in the Day-Ahead Market on the Large Load’s behalf or voluntarily 
choose to be served in real-time at the Real-Time LBMP.  
The NYISO is proposing to settle Large Loads with Firm Withdrawal Service for Energy 
in substantially the same manner as existing Loads receiving firm transmission service are settled 
for Energy today. 
ii. 
Limited Withdrawal Service Loads 
“Limited Withdrawal Service Loads” are Large Loads that are taking some amount of  
Non-Firm Withdrawal Service and/or are, in part or in full, a Zero Withdrawal Service Load. 
This includes Large Loads that are taking, in part but not in full, some amount of Firm 
Withdrawal Service.  Limited Withdrawal Service Loads are: (i) completely or partially excluded 
from the Load forecasts used to establish the IRM and LCRs; (ii) therefore subject to some 
amount of the System Reliability Benefits Fee; (iii) submit (negative) Energy withdrawal bids to 
consume Energy from the grid or, when co-located with supply, a single net Energy bid; (iv) are 
ineligible to register as Demand Side Resources; and (v) are required to participate in the 
proposed CLLC participation model. The CLLC participation model is a single unified result that 
can simultaneously support both the standalone and co-located participation of Limited 
Withdrawal Service Loads. Though the CLLC ruleset is singular, there are specific stipulations 
for Zero Withdrawal Service Load and NFWS Load contained therein. To the extent that a 
Limited Withdrawal Service Load is also a Co-Located Local Load, it will be modeled as 
belonging to a Co-Located Local Generator Configuration (“CLGC”) that neither withdraws 
from nor injects onto the system. 
a) 
Exclusion of Limited Withdrawal Service Large Loads from 
Capacity Forecasts and Capacity Markets 
 
492 Zachary T. Smith Affidavit at P 16 (“Smith Affidavit”).

PDF page 87

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 87 
 
 
Large Loads taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service will be excluded from the Load 
Forecasts used in establishing the Installed Reserve Margin and Locational Minimum Installed 
Capacity Requirement.493  Accordingly, the NYISO will not be obligated to serve Large Loads 
taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service during peak hours, and such Large Loads will have no right 
to consume Energy during times of peak demand.   Unlike Firm Withdrawal Service, Large 
Loads taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service will not be eligible to participate in NYISO Demand 
Side Resource programs.494 
It is appropriate to exclude Large Loads or the portion thereof that are receiving Non-
Firm Withdrawal Service or are ZWS Load from capacity-related Load Forecasts because they 
do not contribute to their LSEs’ minimum ICAP market obligations, and their demand does not 
clear in the ICAP market auctions. No entity will procure ICAP on behalf of these 
aforementioned types of Load through the ICAP market auctions, or through other means, like a 
Capacity market bilateral. As a result, these Large Loads are excluded from traditional ICAP 
charges.   However, these Large Loads still benefit from the stability provided by Capacity 
Resources in hours in which the Large Load is eligible to withdraw Energy from the 
transmission system, despite these Large Loads having not cleared in the ICAP market auction. 
Therefore, the NYISO will require Large Loads or the portions thereof that are taking Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service or are ZWS Load to pay a System Reliability Benefit Fee as discussed 
below in Part IV.B.ii.g.  These requirements help to protect customers by incentivizing these 
Large Loads to voluntarily take the measures necessary to obtain Full Withdrawal Rights.  
Because Large Loads receiving Non-Firm Withdrawal Service are non-firm customers, it 
is appropriate that the NYISO is not obliged to redispatch to continue service to them until the 
curtailment threshold described in Part IV.B.ii.b below is reached.  It is likewise reasonable that 
such Large Loads have no right to consume Energy during such times.  
b) 
The Non-Firm Bid Cap and Out-of-Merit Requirements for Limited 
Withdrawal Service Loads 
The NYISO proposes to add new language to Section 21 (Attachment F) of the Services 
Tariff which governs bidding restrictions.  Proposed new Section 21.4.7, “Large Loads 
Participating in a CLLC” would establish that “[t]he portion of a Large Load participating in a 
CLLC that is taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service shall be subject to a $200/MWh maximum 
Bid Price . . . .”  The proposed tariff provision notes that this “is the mechanism by which these 
Large Loads are interruptible/curtailed to avoid adverse impacts to the grid.”  By contrast, Large 
Loads with Firm Withdrawal Service will not be subject to the proposed bid cap.  They will be 
curtailed on the same basis and with the same priority as firm transmission service loads that 
exist in the NYISO today, i.e., only for reliability emergencies per NYISO’s existing emergency 
procedures.   
The NYISO is also proposing to include language in new OATT Section 3A.1.2 to 
establish that Large Loads taking Non-Firm Withdrawal Service “shall timely respond to ISO or

Transmission Owner Out-of-Merit instructions, including instructions to reduce withdrawals or 
disconnect when necessary to maintain system reliability, consistent with ISO Procedures.” 
Explanation of the NYISO’s Proposal 
 
NYISO currently uses Constraint Specific Shortage Pricing (“CSSP”) to represent the 
price impact that transmission shortages have on LBMPs. The CSSP is supported by a six-step 
Transmission Demand Curve representing transmission shortage prices that increase in tandem 
with further utilization of a transmission facility’s Constraint Reliability Margin (“CRM”). The 
CRM is a portion of transmission facility transmission capacity that the NYISO has reserved 
between a predetermined market limit and the facility’s hard limit, i.e. its line rating, which 
serves to insulate the NYCA transmission system from severe overload risk by allowing 
transmission scarcity to be reflected in market prices. In times of severe transmission scarcity, 
i.e. when a transmission facility reaches 100% CRM utilization, flows across a transmission 
facility will near or meet the facility’s line rating and the facility’s Transmission Demand Curve 
will produce a transmission constraint price of $4,000/MWh.  
The NERC Transmission Loading Relief (“TLR”) process is used to filter/sort external 
transactions for curtailment, based on a transaction’s ability to affect flows across a given 
flowgate.  This process uses an individual transaction quantity-share de minimis threshold of 5% 
when determining whether the curtailment of an individual transaction will materially impact 
flows across a given flowgate. The NYISO applied similar materiality logic to the CSSP 
construct, specifically to the Transmission Demand Curve, in determining the $200/MWh bid 
price cap for NFWS Loads. 
The NYISO will use the 5% materiality threshold used in the NERC TLR process in 
determining which NFWS Loads have material impact on flows across a binding transmission 
constraint, i.e. an NFWS Load that has a minimum Shift Factor of 5% on a transmission 
constraint will be considered to have material impact on flows across that constraint. As 
discussed, when the Transmission Demand Curve binds at 100% CRM utilization, it produces a 
price of $4,000/MWh on the associated transmission constraint. NYISO believes this is the point 
at which it becomes most appropriate to ensure NFWS Loads that exacerbate this constraint are 
curtailed. An NFWS Load whose Shift Factor meets the 5% materiality threshold described 
above would, except in rare cases, receive a nodal price of $200/MWh when the correlated 
transmission constraint produces a $4,000/MWh price. By aligning the NFWS Load bid cap with 
this $200/MWh nodal LBMP, NYISO ensures that NFWS Loads that meet the 5% materiality 
threshold are curtailed automatically by the market optimization when the CRM of a relevant 
transmission facility is fully utilized, preventing the Large Load from exacerbating the constraint 
further and avoiding manual intervention by NYISO Grid Operators.  
The proposed bid cap ensures that Non-Firm Withdrawal Service Large Loads will be 
curtailed in advance of significant transmission security or resource adequacy shortage events.  It 
also increases the probability that Non-Firm Withdrawal Service Loads that most aggravate a 
given transmission constraint, i.e. those with higher Shift Factors on a given constraint, will be 
curtailed in advance of those that do not.  This results in a natural precipitation of NFWS Load 
curtailment priority that is grounded directly in reliability and automated by NYISO’s

PDF page 89

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 89 
 
 
optimization. It will benefit customers by helping to prevent Large Loads from driving up 
LBMPs and will also help to reduce the likelihood of reliability issues.  
Some stakeholders have suggested that a single $200/MWh bid cap would be 
insufficiently flexible and could be too low of a dollar figure especially in times of high winter 
fuel costs.  However as the Smith Affidavit explains, it will not be feasible for the NYISO to 
adopt fuel indexing for the bid cap, or for other potentially relevant market parameters, in the 
near-term, especially in the expedited timeframe that the Commission has allowed for responses 
to the Show Cause Order.495  Additionally, during the course of the stakeholder sessions, the 
argument has also been made that the $200/MWh Bid cap may be set below the non-firm Large 
Load's true cost of curtailment and therefore could lead to inefficient pricing signals. However, 
this is not a persuasive reason to opt for a different non-firm Bid price cap level for several 
reasons, including the fact that the Large Load has voluntarily elected non-firm service, which is 
a lower level of service compared to firm service.  
Use of a bid cap to effectuate the necessary curtailment of non-firm service is an 
effective, fair, and efficient implementing mechanism. Moreover, the claim, that the threshold 
may be set below the non-firm Load’s true cost of curtailment could be true of all bid cap levels, 
including alternative bid caps of $1000/MWh or $2000/MWh.  It is possible that many Limited 
Withdrawal Service Loads would be willing to consume above the $200/MWh threshold. 
However, allowing Large Loads to offer in at any level under an alternative Bid cap of 
$1000/MWh or $2000/MWh would lead to distorted outcomes and market signals. First, any 
curtailments that the NYISO would need to effectuate in order to properly ensure reliability 
would be required to be handled outside the market software, in an “ad hoc” manner, and could 
result in there being schedules that are inconsistent with prices. Second, it is possible that many 
of these non-firm loads would remain willing to consume above even $2000/MWh As such, the 
NYISO contends that the $200/MWh Bid Cap proposal is necessary to protect reliability, and is 
just and reasonable.    
In order to effectuate this price cap, and to ensure that Large Loads taking NFWS are 
responding to the $200/MWh nodal LBMP curtailment threshold in Real-Time as mandated by 
the terms of their Withdrawal Service, NYISO proposes to model these Loads as negative 
generation, akin to the way it models the Energy Withdrawal portion of an Energy Storage 
Resource (“ESR”). This modeling approach extends to all types of Limited Withdrawal Service 
Loads, i.e. all Loads that participate using the CLLC participation model. This means that, like a 
Generator, the Market Participant representing a Limited Withdrawal Service Load will need to 
submit an 11-point incremental Energy Withdrawal bid to the NYISO’s market optimization in 
each interval in which the Load seeks to consume Energy from the grid. Like an ESR that 
submits an Energy Withdrawal Bid, the Large Load’s bids will be evaluated by the optimization, 
and the Load will subsequently be dispatched for discrete amounts of Energy Withdrawals 
according to the economics of their bids and the physical characteristics of the Load. The Market 
Participant may, but is not required to, specify multiple $/MWh price points in its Energy 
Withdrawal Bid that represent the NFWS Load’s willingness to pay for Energy at different price 
 
495 See Smith Affidavit at P 25.

PDF page 90

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 90 
 
 
points below the $200/MWh NFWS nodal LBMP bid cap. Doing so effectively expands the 
price sensitivity of the NFWS Load.  
The Smith Affidavit emphasizes that the NYISO’s proposed rules and requirements for 
Non-Firm Withdrawal Service Large Loads reflect a “a reasonable, market-based approach that 
is consistent with the NYISO’s broader market design proposal and existing rules and 
frameworks.”496 The newly proposed Non-Firm Withdrawal Service should be found to be just 
and reasonable, as it provides Large Loads with an option to connect to the grid faster and 
without the potential delays required to mitigate adverse impacts to resource adequacy and/or 
transmission security. 
 
The Harvey Affidavit provides additional support for the market design, operational, and 
reliability advantages of the curtailment proposal.497  Dr. Harvey explains that the NYISO’s 
proposal functions very similarly to the Commission-approved Southwest Power Pool (“SPP”) 
non-firm service curtailment rules and also appears to be consistent with proposals made in the 
PJM Co-Location Order proceedings.498  At the same time, the NYISO’s proposal is “more 
concrete and amenable to modeling in unit commitment and interchange scheduling software, as 
well as real-time dispatch software than the general hierarchy of load shedding articulated by 
PJM or the general criteria in the SPP tariff.”499 In addition, “the NYISO price-based curtailment 
threshold is also forward looking and could identify the need to reduce non-firm load not just 
immediately before shedding of firm load is necessary, but identify the need to reduce non-firm 
load in order to help assure that shedding of firm load will not become necessary in future hours 
because of the impact of meeting that non-firm load on gas availability or battery state of 
charge.”500   
 
Dr. Harvey also contends that the NYISO’s proposed use of a price based curtailment 
trigger would provide greater transparency to Large Loads served via Non-Firm Withdrawal 
Service501 and be more consistent with the overall evolution of organized markets away from 
reliance on operator judgment and towards the use of sophisticated modeling tools.502  He 
maintains that relying on a price-based curtailment rule will reinforce reliability, help to 
maximize the utilization of the transmission system, and be practically administrable by the 
NYISO. 
 
Moreover, Dr. Harvey supports the NYISO’s selection of a $200/MWh bid price cap 
level as “reasonable and appropriate from a number of perspectives” including market pricing 
considerations, compatibility with market initiatives that the NYISO may pursue in the future, 
and suitability for the evolving resource mix.503

The Curtailment Proposal Is Commission-Jurisdictional 
 
The NYISO is mindful that there questions may be raised regarding the jurisdictional 
status of its curtailment proposal. In particular, the Commission recently rejected substantial 
portions of PJM’s proposal to introduce an “Interim Resource Adequacy Service” (“IRAS”) and 
Bring Your Own New Capacity framework in its region.504  PJM defined IRAS as “a load 
reduction service through which New Large Loads reduce load as directed by the applicable 
Electric Distributor[.]”505 Among other determinations, the Commission found that PJM’s 
proposed tariff language appeared to be setting a retail rate because it would have established a 
“compensation rate for IRAS payable to the Eligible New Large Load.”506  Notably, the 
Commission stated that “a non-firm transmission service similar to Non-Firm Contract Demand 
transmission service, with appropriate adjustments, might be considered as a potential avenue to 
achieve some of the aims of PJM’s proposed IRAS framework.”507 
By contrast, the NYISO neither proposes to mandate any load reduction service of the 
kind presented IRAS  or otherwise, nor proposes any form of compensation for such a service. 
Rather, the NYISO is proposing to provide Transmission Customers taking service on behalf of 
Large Loads and Large Loads acting as their own LSEs with a new Commission-jurisdictional 
non-firm transmission service option with attributes similar to Non-Firm Contract Demand 
service, with appropriate adjustments,508 which aligns with the Commission’s guidance set forth 
in the IRAS Order. 
Unlike PJM under IRAS, the NYISO would not curtail NITS, or any other form of firm 
transmission service, to similarly situated firm loads.  Instead, the NYISO is proposing a new 
category of service to differently situated loads (i.e., loads voluntarily choosing to be served via  
Non-Firm Withdrawal Service instead of  Firm Withdrawal Service.)  The possibility of 
curtailment is a necessary component of Non-Firm Withdrawal Service because it ensures the 
reliability of the NYISO’s wholesale markets and transmission system during elevated demand 
conditions.509 A Large Load receiving Non-Firm Withdrawal Service will have been studied and 
found to have adverse impacts resource adequacy impacts that it has chosen not to mitigate.  The 
NYISO would not simply be relegating the Large Load to inferior service based on 
characteristics that touch on retail jurisdictional domains.  
Additionally, a CLLC is not entitled to compensation when it is curtailed under Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service. Mandatory curtailment of Limited Withdrawal Service Loads when 
capacity exceeds the nodal $200/MWh price threshold is required to avoid adverse impacts to 
resource adequacy and/or transmission security. The NYISO’s proposed bid cap is a condition to 
 
504 PJM Interconnection, L.L.C., 197 FERC ¶ 61,019 (2026) (“PJM IRAS Order”).  
505 Id. at P 81. 
506 Id. at P 82. 
507 Id. at P 111. 
508 As is discussed in Part V, below, the NYISO is not proposing to adopt the Non-Firm Contract Demand 
Service in the form specified in the PJM Co-Location Order. 
509 Cf. id. P 91 (“Data Center Coalition and Vistra also argue that PJM misread the SPP CHILLS precedent, 
which they argue supports offering different services to differently situated loads but not reducing NITS for Electric 
Distributors serving materially identical loads.”)

PDF page 92

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 92 
 
 
the receipt of Commission-jurisdictional non-firm transmission service and is a wholesale market 
design mechanism that exists in the wholesale energy space.  The proposed NYISO tariff 
obligation runs to the CLLC (or a Transmission Customer taking service on its behalf) as a 
wholesale market participant, the wholesale market dictates whether the Large Load will be 
curtailed, and the NYISO does not impose any charge on a retail customer.  The NYISO’s 
proposal falls within the bounds of the Commission’s jurisdiction.  And the NYISO’s proposal 
does not impermissibly intrude on state authority 510 
The NYISO’s curtailment proposal is much closer to SPP’s Conditional High Impact 
Large Load Service (“CHILLS”), which the Commission accepted earlier this year,511 than it is 
to IRAS.  SPP proposed CHILLS as a new, non-firm transmission service that would enable high 
impact large loads to take transmission service prior to having the sufficient designated resources 
or network upgrades in place that are required for long-term, firm service.512 CHILLS is subject 
to curtailment and interruption on a non-discriminatory basis when the transmission system is 
constrained.513 CHILLS has equal curtailment priority to SPP’s monthly non-firm point-to-point 
transmission service.514  As noted in Part V, the NYISO does not currently offer any form of 
non-firm transmission service but Non-Firm Withdrawal Service, like CHILLS and pro forma 
OATT non-firm service, would be curtailed before firm transmission service in NYISO.   
The NYISO’s proposal shares many features that the Commission found significant in 
accepting CHILLS. As with CHILLS, the NYISO’s Non-Firm Withdrawal Service is a new non-
firm service and curtailment is an integral part of that service priority level. The curtailment 
obligation also is implemented through an objective standard that would be part of the NYISO’s 
Commission-jurisdictional wholesale market framework as opposed to curtailment via a 
discretionary directive to a retail customer. There is also no retail compensation mechanism for 
curtailment.  The CHILLS Order is thus precedent for the NYISO’s Non-Firm Withdrawal 
Service curtailment rule being subject to the Commission’s jurisdiction.   
Finally, as the NYISO noted with respect to the State Exempt Large Load Project 
proposal, courts have found that the intent underlying a regulatory requirement is an important 
part of drawing jurisdictional lines under the FPA’s cooperative federalism framework.  Here 
again, the NYISO has no intention of interfering with or intruding into New York State 
jurisdiction.  The NYISO is instead adapting well-established Commission-jurisdictional rules 
allowing transmission providers to curtail Commission-jurisdictional non-firm services to fit the 
NYISO’s Large Load market participation model.

c) 
Excluding Limited Withdrawal Service Loads from Participation as 
Demand Side Resources 
The NYISO is proposing to exclude Limited Withdrawal Service Loads, i.e., Loads that 
are not fully served via Firm Withdrawal Service, from participation in the ISO Administered 
Markets as a Demand Side Resource (e.g., Special Case Resource (“SCR”), Emergency Demand 
Response Program (“EDRP”), Distributed Energy Resource (“DER”) and Aggregations).515  It is 
reasonable to make Large Loads taking NFWS ineligible to receive compensation for demand 
reductions because Large Loads taking this service have voluntarily elected to take a lower level 
of transmission service in order to either avoid paying for Network Upgrades or to avoid 
bringing new ICAP supply to market to mitigate any resource adequacy and/or transmission 
security impacts they may cause, as determined in the LLIP.  Non-Firm Withdrawal Service 
Large Loads, by definition, are not entitled to consume Energy from the system at any time. 
Accordingly, NFWS Large Loads are not in a position to voluntarily reduce their consumption of 
Energy from the grid at all, let alone do so during the specific hours in which there would be a 
net benefit to the system and/or other customers by virtue of their voluntary demand reduction.516 
It follows that Large Loads taking NFWS are excluded from the Load forecasts that 
inform the Installed Reserve Margin (“IRM”) and Locational Minimum Installed Capacity 
Requirements (“LCRs”), do not create an ICAP obligation for their LSE and therefore Capacity 
Resources are not procured on their behalf through the ICAP auctions.  Large Loads with NFWS 
are not similarly situated to those with Firm Withdrawal Service. 
When a Large Load with Firm Withdrawal Service voluntarily registers to participate in 
the ISO Administered Markets as a Demand Side Resource, it is agreeing to forego its Firm 
Withdrawal Service Rights, i.e. agreeing to voluntarily curtail its Load, in exchange for payment 
when the requisite net benefit threshold is met. This payment is justified because the absence of a 
Large Load making its typical withdrawals from the system via its Firm Withdrawal Service 
during peak hours reduces NYISO’s net on-peak Capacity obligations and when the net benefits 
test is met and hence, the overall benefit of the reduced LBMP that results from dispatching such 
FWS Large Loads to reduce their consumption exceeds the cost of dispatching the more 
expensive generation resources.517  In other words, by qualifying as an ICAP Supplier and 
offering ICAP at auction, a Firm Withdrawal Service Load is reducing the net impact that its 
Load has on the outcomes of the ICAP market auctions, and reduces its LSE’s net ICAP 
obligations as a result.  
In sum, Large Loads with Firm Withdrawal Service may elect to participate as a Demand 
Side Resource in the NYISO markets and thus receive compensation for voluntary demand 
reductions during hours in which there is a net benefit to other customers and the system for 
 
515 See, e.g., proposed Services Tariff § 2.4. 
516 See Demand Response Compensation in Organized Wholesale Energy Markets, Order No. 745, 76 Fed. 
Reg. 16,658 (Mar. 24, 2011), FERC Stats. & Regs. ¶ 31,322 (2011) (“Order 745”). “Rather than requiring 
compensation at LMP in all hours, the Commission requires the use of the net benefits test described herein to 
ensure that the overall benefit of the reduced LMP that results from dispatching demand response resources exceeds 
the cost of dispatching those resources.” Id. at P 150 (emphasis added). 
517 See Order 745 at P 150.

PDF page 94

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 94 
 
 
them to do so. Whereas Large Loads with Non-Firm Withdrawal Service have elected to receive 
a lower level of transmission service and are not entitled to withdraw, or refrain from 
withdrawing, in exchange for demand reduction compensation. This justification extends to 
ZWS Loads as well, which are Large Loads that lack any right to withdraw from the system, or 
voluntarily refrain from withdrawing, altogether.  Non-firm Large Loads are far from being 
similarly situated to Large Loads with Firm service.  
d) 
Market Participation Provisions Applicable to Limited Withdrawal 
Service Loads that Receive “Partial” Firm Withdrawal Service  
As previously stated, Large Loads taking some amount of Firm Withdrawal Service 
along with some amount of Non-Firm Withdrawal Service and/or that are partially Zero 
Withdrawal Service Loads, will be classified as Limited Withdrawal Service Load and must 
participate under the CLLC participation model. Like with other CLLCs, these “partial” Firm 
Withdrawal Service Large Loads will still be required to submit Energy Bids in the Day-Ahead 
Market whenever they seek to withdraw from the grid and will need to be dispatchable. It will be 
possible for Market Participants to “preserve” their Firm Withdrawal rights when participating 
under the CLLC participation model through the structuring of their bids, such that CLLC 
scheduling outcomes honor the Large Load’s right to in part consume Energy from the grid 
regardless of price.518     
e) 
Market Rules Applicable to Zero Withdrawal Service Loads 
(“ZWSL”)   
The NYISO is proposing to add new language to the Services Tariff to address the 
treatment of ZWSLs.  The introduction of a defined ZWSL voluntary option and related market 
rules is responsive to the Show Cause Order’s observation that the NYISO’s tariffs did “not 
appear to include a mechanism by which an Eligible Customer, on behalf of Eligible Load, 
would be charged for ancillary services commensurate with its usage of such services, 
particularly if such customer did not withdraw energy from the transmission system.”519  The 
ZWSL proposal is also compatible and practically implementable within the framework of the 
NYISO’s market design and financial reservation transmission model.    
The new ZWSL designation applies to a Large Load or portion thereof that is not 
permitted to physically withdraw Energy from the NYS Transmission System or has requested 
not to withdraw Energy from the NYS Transmission System and is served exclusively by a Co-
Located Resource(s) or Co-Located Local Generator.  The ZWSL designation is only relevant to 
co-located configurations, i.e., Large Load that cannot be served by front-of-the-meter 
generation or that a Market Participant has requested not be served by front-of-the-meter 
generation. ZWSL are not studied for and do not receive eligibility to receive any Withdrawal 
Services through the LLIP, i.e., do not have a right to withdraw from the grid to serve their Load.   
ZWSL status is similar to the BTM:NG Host Load concept under NYISO’s existing 
design, in that ZWSL is Load that exists behind-the-meter and is served exclusively by behind-
 
518 See Smith Affidavit at P 68. 
519 Show Cause Order at P 106.

PDF page 95

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 95 
 
 
the-meter generation, which may or may not offer excess generator capability to the system via 
participation in the CLLC participation model.  BTM:NG Host Loads effectively function and 
participate in the NYISO markets under this construct today.  
Co-Located Local Generators are providing service behind the meter to ZWSL and are 
not requesting, or permitted to inject any Energy onto the system.  They are therefore not 
required to obtain Energy Resource Interconnection Service (“ERIS”) or CRIS.  These Co-
Located Local Generators may still request ERIS and/or CRIS rights if they are able and willing 
to offer excess operating capacity supply into the market that is not needed to support the ZWSL.    
A Large Load that is a full ZWSL that is supplied solely by a co-located Generator(s) that 
lacks ERIS/CRIS and exists solely to serve the Zero Withdrawal Service Load are respectively 
referred to as a Co-Located Local Load and a Co-Located Local Generator.  If the resulting co-
located configuration is interconnected and synchronized to the NYS Transmission System, the 
configuration will be classified as a Co-Located Local Generator Configuration (“CLGC”). Such 
a configuration does not interface with the NYISO markets but, consistent with the general rule 
for ZWSL, would be subject to the Gross Load cost allocation methodology described in Part 
IV.B.ii.f and to the System Reliability Benefit Fee described in Part IV.B.ii.g. 
The ZWSL option may be attractive if it aligns with potential market participant’s 
business objectives or use cases, e.g., if the value of a faster development timeline makes it 
worthwhile for a Large Load to elect to take this option.520  For example, if a Co-Located 
Facility’s Point of Withdrawal is undersized relative to the amount of Load that is seeking to 
interconnect behind-the-meter, some or all of that Load may choose to classify as ZWSL and be 
served exclusively by behind-the-meter generation. 
ZWS Load is subject to the Gross Load cost allocation methodology described in Part 
IV.B.ii.f as it is, by definition, interconnected and synchronized to the NYS Transmission 
System and therefore benefits from the stability provided by Ancillary and Transmission 
Services. ZWS Load is additionally subject to the SRBF discussed in Part IV.B.ii.g. This is 
consistent with the Show Cause Order’s expressed concern that the NYISO’s Tariff does not 
appear to include a “mechanism by which an Eligible Customer [] would be charged for ancillary 
services commensurate with its usage of such services, particularly if such customer did not 
withdraw energy from the transmission system.”521 
As such, the NYISO’s proposal to provide a participation framework for Zero 
Withdrawal Service Loads appropriately accommodates the unique operating characteristics of 
this type of Large Load and is just and reasonable. 
f) 
Allocating Costs to Large Loads on a Gross Load Basis 
The Show Cause Order preliminarily found that the NYISO’s tariff appears to be unjust 
and unreasonable because it does not address whether or how Eligible Customers taking 
transmission services on behalf of Eligible Loads will be charged for their use of regulation and 
 
520 Smith Affidavit at P 40. 
521 Show Cause Order at PP 106-107.

PDF page 96

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 96 
 
 
black start services, creating a risk that Eligible Load may benefit from these services without 
contributing to cost recovery for such services.522  In response to the Commission’s concern, and 
to ensure that Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of Eligible Load pay for 
wholesale transmission services that they receive, consistent with the cost causation principle,523 
the NYISO proposes to assess fees and charges to all Large Loads on a gross Load basis.524  This 
treatment will include, but is not limited to, assessment on a Gross Load Basis of Operating 
Reserves Charges, Black Start Charges, Voltage Support Service Charges, and Regulation 
Service Charges.525  The NYISO would implement this change via proposed amendments to 
OATT Sections 6.2, 6.3, and 6.5.   
Applying these charges to all Large Loads on a Gross Load basis, regardless of 
Withdrawal Rights, rather than on a Net Load basis, will help to prevent unreasonable cost 
shifting to existing Loads and thus to protect customers.  
g) 
The System Reliability Benefit Fee 
The NYISO proposes to assess a System Reliability Benefit Fee (“SRBF”) under new 
OATT Section 6.24.  The SRBF would be “assessed to an LSE associated with the MW of (a) 
the portion of a Limited Withdrawal Service Load(s) that is not taking Firm Withdrawal Service 
and/or (b) a Co-Located Local Load(s) that is not included in the applicable LSE’s portion of 
Unforced Capacity Obligation the NYCA Minimum Unforced Capacity Requirement, in 
accordance with Section 5.11.1 of the Services Tariff.” 
The purpose of the SRBF is to allocate ICAP market costs Limited Withdrawal Service 
Loads, or portions thereof, not taking Firm Withdrawal Service in an amount roughly 
commensurate to the benefits they receive from ICAP Resources and the value of the reliability 
service that they provide during periods of low demand.  The SRBF will serve as a cost allocator 
for these Large Loads, which will benefit from reliable service despite not being required to pay 
ICAP market costs.    
As discussed in the Smith Affidavit,526 the SRBF will be assessed to the LSE taking 
service on behalf of a Limited Withdrawal Large Service Load.527  It is not a volumetric charge 
based on actual Large Load energy withdrawals, Instead, it will be based on the amount of Non-
Firm Withdrawal Service or Zero Withdrawal Service MW studied and authorized through the 
LLIP.  By tying the SRBF to authorized MW rather than actual MWh, the NYISO will charge 
these Large Loads for the reliability benefits they receive from the NYISO capacity market. 
Without the SRBF, these Large Loads would benefit from stable and reliable system operations 
without contributing correspondingly to the costs incurred to maintain those operations. The

should not be construed as a concession that the NYISO’s existing tariffs are unjust, unreasonable, or unduly 
discriminatory or preferential   
525 See Proposed OATT §§ 6.2, 6.3, and 6.5.

assign these residuals to such LSEs based on LSEs’ share of the total NYCA-wide ICAP

obligation.528  This methodology will mitigate LSEs’ Capacity costs as well as, potentially, costs

The SRBF proposal is responsive to the Show Cause Order’s preliminary finding that the 
NYISO’s Tariff appears to be unjust and unreasonable because it “lacks adequate mechanisms to 
mitigate the risk of cost shifting among transmission customers, which may result in unjust and 
unreasonable rates for transmission service.”529 
C. 
Proposed Large Load Operating Requirements 
The NYISO proposes to revise Sections 3.5.2 and 13 of the Services Tariff to require 
Transmission Customers taking service on behalf of Large Loads to submit the following 
information to the NYISO: (i) six-second Real-Time telemetry; (ii) hourly revenue-grade meter 
data; (iii) Day-Ahead and hourly real-time load forecasts; (iv) PMU data and Phase 1 metering, 
as applicable; (v) separate and direct metering of Load at co-located facilities; and (vi) other 
information and data in accordance with ISO Procedures.  Transmission Customers will also be 
required to submit all data, telemetry, operating information, and control documentation for 
Large Loads on a facility-specific basis.530 These requirements ensure that the NYISO has 
necessary operational visibility into and control over Large Loads, which will result in more 
accurate and reliable market solutions and operator decisions. Having clear operational 
requirements in place also protects customer interests by minimizing the need for costly out-of-
market actions to account for changes in Large Load behavior.  
As discussed in the Smith Affidavit, the NYISO’s proposal will expressly preserve 
Connecting Transmission Owners’ (“CTOs”) authority to remotely disconnect Large Loads 
when necessary to maintain reliability of the transmission system.531  It would also allow the 
NYISO to request that a CTO remotely disconnect a Large Load for reliability reasons.  In 
addition, the NYISO and/or CTO may request disconnection of a CLLC or a CLGC to avoid 
impermissible injections and/or withdrawals.  These actions are expected to be consistent with 
existing operating and communication procedures that exist today with transmission owners. 532 
This proposal is responsive to the Show Cause Order’s directive that the NYISO’s Tariff 
should include provisions addressing ongoing operational requirements for transmission 
customers taking transmission service on behalf of large load.533 The Commission specifically 
highlighted the absence of provisions requiring transmission customers to provide the NYISO 
hourly forecasts, telemetry data, and other data necessary to ensure sufficient visibility, as well 
as requirements for maintaining communication channels between the NYISO and the 
Transmission Customers on behalf of the large load.534

allocated to LSEs based on their share of the applicable NYCA Minimum Unforced Capacity Requirement.”).

D. 
Additional CLLC Rules 
i. 
Overview and Eligibility 
As noted above, the NYISO is proposing to establish and describe a new market 
participation model in the Services Tariff for Co-Located Arrangements535—the Co-Located and 
Limited Load Configuration (“CLLC”) framework. 
 
ii. 
CLLC-Specific Definitions 
The NYISO proposes several definitions related to co-location. In particular, the NYISO 
proposes to define CLLC as: 
A configuration that consists of (a) a Co-Located Load that is a 
Limited Withdrawal Service Load participating with one or more 
Co-Located Resources, in accordance with Section 4.1.12 of the 
ISO Services Tariff, or (b) a stand-alone Limited Withdrawal 
Service Load. All components of the CLLC must be located 
behind a single Point of Interconnection. A Large Load taking only 
Firm Withdrawal Service may not participate in a CLLC. 
A Co-Located Resource(s) participating in a CLLC may not 
participate in the ISO Administered Markets through any other 
participation model. A Co-Located Resource that is a Hybrid 
Storage Resource cannot participate in a CLLC.  
The CLLC shall participate in the ISO-administered Energy and 
Ancillary Services markets as a single Resource that is capable of 
following the ISO’s dispatch instructions. In order to qualify for 
wholesale market participation, a CLLC must be able to inject or 
withdraw at least 1 MW of Energy to/from the transmission 
system. A CLLC will be required to submit Energy Bids consistent 
with the Withdrawal Service(s) being taken by the Co-Located 
Load and the manner in which the Market Participant seeks to 
exercise those Withdrawal Service rights within a given real-time 
market interval. The Co-Located Resource(s) in a CLLC shall 
participate in the ISO-administered Installed Capacity market as a 
single ICAP Supplier referred to as a Composite Co-Located 
Resource.536 
 
535 A Co-Located Arrangement is an agreement between a Co-Located Load and a Co-Located Resource(s) 
that are connected behind the same Point of Interconnection, which is entered into for the purpose of enabling the 
Co-Located Load to take some amount of Firm Withdrawal Service.  
536 Proposed Services Tariff Section 2.3.

PDF page 100

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 100 
 
 
The NYISO also proposes the following CLLC-related definitions:537 
Co-Located Load: A Large Load connected behind the same 
Point of Interconnection as a Co-Located Resource. A Co-Located 
Load may include an ESR in a CLLC in which all withdrawals of 
the ESR are settled as Load, in accordance with Section 4.1.12.1 of 
the ISO Services Tariff. 
Co-Located Resource: A Resource connected behind the same 
Point of Interconnection as a Co-Located Load. A Co-Located 
Resource may include an Energy Limited Resource; an Energy 
Storage Resource; a Generator without a PURPA contract(s); a 
Hybrid Storage Resource; an Intermittent Power Resource; a 
Limited Control Run of River Hydro Resource; and a Demand 
Side Resource that is participating as a standalone Distributed 
Energy Resource. A Co-Located Local Generator is not a Co-
Located Resource. 
Composite Co-Located Resource: One or more Co-Located 
Resource(s) within a CLLC that participates in the ISO-
administered Installed Capacity market as a single Installed 
Capacity Supplier. 
Withdrawal Scheduling Limit: The maximum amount of MW 
associated with a CLLC that is registered as eligible for withdrawal 
scheduling by the NYISO. 
Zero Withdrawal Service Load: A Large Load or portion thereof 
that is not authorized under the ISO OATT to withdraw Energy 
from the NYS Transmission System. A Zero Withdrawal Service 
Load is either (a) a Limited Withdrawal Service Load in a CLLC 
or (b) a Co-Located Local Load in a CLGC. Energy consumption 
by Zero Withdrawal Service Loads shall be metered and included 
in Gross Load. 
The NYISO proposes to clarify that an ESR, as defined in Services Tariff Section 2.5, 
that participates in a CLLC shall follow the rules set forth in Section 4.1.12.1 of the Services 
Tariff.”538 The NYISO also proposes conforming revisions to the definitions of Resource, 
Supplier, Installed Capacity Supplier, Lower Operating Limit, Minimum Generation Bid and 
Minimum Generation Level, and Station Power to integrate the CLLC. 
The NYISO’s definition of Co-Located Resource is broad, encompassing Resource types 
such as HSR, CSR, and others. This is intentional – the Co-Located Resource definition is 
intended to be applied broadly to contexts external to the CLLC participation model, such as the 
 
537 Id. 
538 Id.

PDF page 101

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 101 
 
 
Co-Located Arrangement construct. A subset of Co-Located Resources, i.e. Generators that do 
not utilize other participation models, will be eligible to participate in a CLLC.  For example, 
storage is a CLLC-eligible technology type, but storage participating in a CLLC will not be able 
to utilize the NYISO’s Energy Storage Resource participation model rules. This is to prevent 
Market Participants from creating nested Aggregations, such as locating an HSR within a CLLC, 
and to maintain a distinct participation model that can support the integration of Load and 
Generation into a single co-located facility. 
Demand Side Resources are ineligible to participate in the CLLC participation model by 
default. In order to participate in a CLLC, any composite Load would have to be classified as 
Limited Withdrawal Service Load, which disqualifies the Load from qualifying for Demand 
Response compensation. However, Load flexibility can still be reflected in the CLLC’s net 
Energy bid, and may be reflected in other aspects of CLLC performance such as DMNC tests. 
This enables the CLLC Market Participant to utilize Load flexibility in effectuating greater net 
injections onto the grid, in the event that the CLLC contains enough Generation to effectuate 
such an outcome.  
iii. 
CLLC-Specific Metering and Telemetry Requirements 
The NYISO proposes to revise Services Tariff Sections 13.1 (General Requirements) and 
13.2 (Requirements Pertaining to Customers) to extend the metering and six-second real-time 
telemetry requirements to (1) CLLCs and (2) Large Loads, including those in a CLLC or a 
CLGC.539  
The NYISO proposes a new Section 13.2.7 (Metering Requirements for Co-Located and 
Limited Load Configurations). This section would provide that a CLLC shall be metered by the 
Transmission Owner it is interconnected to or in whose Load Zone it is located, and Energy 
injections and withdrawals at the Point of Interconnection shall be metered separately and 
directly and be submitted to the ISO by the Transmission Owner Meter Authority in separate 
fields such that the ISO is able to separately determine the total Energy injections and 
withdrawals in each interval.540 The Co-Located Load shall provide real-time telemetry at the 
Point of Interconnection and the Gross Load at the Large Load nominally every six seconds, in 
accordance with the specifications set forth in ISO Procedures.541

E. 
Deviation Charges for CLLCs that Fail to Follow NYISO’s Dispatch 
Instructions 
The NYISO is proposing to add a new Section 15.3B (“Rate Schedule 3-B”) to the 
Services Tariff to assess new deviation charges to  CLLCs that do not follow the NYISO’s 
dispatch instructions/basepoints when net withdrawing from the system. 
Under Section 15.3B.1 (Overwithdrawal Charges), a CLLC whose Actual Energy 
Withdrawals exceed its scheduled Energy withdrawals over a Real-Time Dispatch interval would 
pay an overwithdrawal charge calculated as follows: 
i. 
Overwithdrawal MW x Max (MPRCDAM, MPRCRT) x Length of Interval in 
seconds 3600 seconds572 
Under Section 15.3B.2 (Underwithdrawal Charges), a CLLC that withdraws less than its 
scheduled Energy withdrawals minus the tolerance will pay a corresponding underwithdrawal 
charge calculated as follows: 
ii. 
Underwithdrawal MW x Max (MPRCDAM, MPRCRT) x Length of Interval in 
seconds 3600 seconds573 
These charges are a necessary incentive mechanism because CLLCs are dispatchable, 
even when net withdrawing from the system. When a Generator today deviates from its 
basepoints, such deviations must be corrected for through the dispatch of additional Regulation 
capacity, which incurs a systemwide cost. Under the existing rules, the additional cost of 
dispatching such Regulation capacity is allocated to the Generators that caused the discrepancy 
via Generator Overgeneration/Undergeneration charges. A CLLC that deviates from its dispatch 
while net withdrawing yields the same end result – NYISO must dispatch additional Regulation 
to compensate for those deviations.  NYISO therefore believes that it is reasonable to assess 
 
569 Id. § 4.5.3.1. 
570 Id. §§ 18.2.1.2, 18.4.2. 
571 See Smith Affidavit at P 79. 
572 Id. § 15.3B.1. 
573 Id.

PDF page 106

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 106 
 
 
similar, but distinct, Overwithdrawal and Underwithdrawal charges to CLLCs in intervals where 
they are dispatched to withdraw yet fail to follow their withdrawal basepoints.  
The CLLC deviation charge that is applied when the CLLC is net withdrawing will have 
a tolerance threshold of 5% of the Large Load’s maximum registered withdrawal limit, i.e. the 
total amount of Withdrawal Service that the Load was studied for and awarded in the LLIP.  The 
charge will be assessed using the Regulation clearing price, consistent with existing 
Under/Overgeneration penalties for Generators. The 5% tolerance applied to the CLLC 
withdrawal deviation charge is more generous than the 3% tolerance applied to Generator 
Overgeneration/Undergeneration charges, and accommodates stakeholder feedback that some 
Loads, though dispatchable, may not be able to modulate Load as precisely as a Generator can 
modulate supply. 
CLLCs will be subject to the existing DER Aggregation Overgeneration/Undergeneration 
charges in market intervals where they are dispatched to net inject onto the system.  The Smith 
Affidavit further addresses the reasonableness of these charges.574 
The Commission should accept the NYISO’s proposed deviation charges as within the 
scope of the Commission’s jurisdiction for the same reasons as the NYISO’s proposed 
curtailment rules for Limited Withdrawal Service Loads, discussed above in Part IV.B.ii.b are 
Commission-jurisdictional.  Namely, the deviation charges are conditions to the receipt of 
jurisdictional non-firm transmission service and are wholesale market design mechanisms that 
exist in the wholesale energy space.  They are intended to incentivize CLLCs’ adherence to 
NYISO dispatch/basepoint instructions under NYISO’s Commission-jurisdictional tariffs.  The 
NYISO has no intention to intrude on state jurisdiction.   
 
In addition, the SPP CHILLS Order also supports NYISO’s proposed deviation charges. 
CHILLS customers taking transmission service in excess of their CHILLS reserved capacity are 
subject to SPP-imposed penalties.575 The Commission found this arrangement to be just and 
reasonable because it ensures that CHILLS customers pay for unreserved use of SPP’s 
transmission system based on the amount and duration of such unreserved use.576 The 
Commission also found that escalating penalties will help deter CHILLS Customers from 
repeatedly failing to adhere to reserved capacity amounts.577 So too with the NYISO’s proposed 
deviation charge, which is structured to ensure that customers being served via Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Service adhere to the NYISO’s dispatch instructions and basepoints when net 
withdrawing. 
 
F. 
The Associated Resource Arrangements and the Capacity Region Construct 
As discussed above in Part II.G.ii, a Large Load that is seeking Firm Withdrawal Service 
but has been identified in the LLIP as causing impermissible adverse resource adequacy impacts 
may mitigate those impacts, and thus be eligible to receive some or a full amount of Firm

Withdrawal Service by entering into an Associated Resource arrangement(s).  The NYISO 
would revise Section 2.1 of the Services Tariff to define an “Associated Resource” as follows: 
[a] Co-Located Resource or Partner Resource that may mitigate some or all of a 
Large Load’s adverse impacts to resource adequacy and/or transmission security 
identified in the LLIP set forth in Attachment LL of the ISO OATT, which allows

Withdrawal Service, consistent with Section 4.1.12 of the ISO Services Tariff and 
Attachment B of the ISO OATT.  The Co-Located Resource(s) and the associated 
Co-Located Load must be behind the same Point of Interconnection and must 
jointly submit an attestation in accordance with Section 44.6.3.5 of Attachment 
LL of the ISO OATT.  A Co-Located Arrangement may be between: (1) a Co-
Located Load taking solely Firm Withdrawal Service and a Co-Located Resource 
or (2) a Co-Located Load and Co-Located Resource in a CLLC. The Co-Located 
Arrangement shall have a required minimum duration of fifteen (15) years, 
starting the first day of the Obligation Procurement Period for which the UCAP 
equivalent of ICAP documented in the Large Load Bilateral Transaction is 
initially certified, in accordance with ISO Services Tariff Sections 5.11.2 and 
5.13.1 and ISO Procedures 
And Services Tariff Section 2.16 would define a “Partnered Resource Arrangement” as: 
An agreement between Partner Resource(s) and a Large Load that are electrically 
located in the same Capacity Region for the purpose of enabling the Large Load 
to take Firm Withdrawal Service, consistent with Section 4.1.12 of the ISO 
Services Tariff and Attachment B of the ISO OATT. The Partner Resource(s) and 
the Large Load must jointly submit an attestation in accordance with Section 
44.6.3.5 of Attachment LL of the ISO OATT. The Partner Resource Arrangement 
shall have a required minimum duration of fifteen (15) years, starting the first day 
of the Obligation Procurement Period for which the UCAP equivalent of ICAP 
documented in the applicable Large Load Bilateral Transaction is initially 
certified, in accordance with Sections 5.11.2 and 5.13.1 of the ISO Services Tariff 
and ISO Procedures.

PDF page 108

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 108 
 
 
Once a Large Load enters into a Co-Located or Partner Resource Arrangement with an 
Associated Resource(s), that Large Load’s eligibility to take the amount of Firm Withdrawal 
Service dependent on such arrangement with an Associated Resource becomes contingent on the 
continued ICAP market participation of the Associated Resource(s).  If the Associated 
Resource(s) that is party to a given Large Load’s Co-Located or Partner Resource Arrangement 
enters into an ICAP Ineligible Forced Outage, Mothball, or Retired status, or otherwise exit the 
ICAP markets, the corresponding Large Load will be required to reclassify, in part or in full, as a 
NFWS Load.  
If the Large Load’s Associated Resource(s) later exit from one of the aforementioned 
statuses and resumes ICAP market participation, then the Large Load’s Firm Withdrawal Service 
Rights may be restored.  
The NYISO proposes to require that all Co-Located and Partner Resource Arrangements 
remain in place for a term of 15 years.578 After the 15-year term expires, the Large Load’s Firm 
Withdrawal Service shall no longer be contingent on the continued ICAP market participation of 
its Associated Resource(s).  
NYISO’s proposed rules ensure that the ICAP associated with an Associated Resource(s) 
in an arrangement with a Large Load is being made available to the ICAP market by requiring 
that a Large Load Bilateral Transaction be in place, which is a capacity bilateral, between 
Associated Resources and the Large Load to whose arrangement they belong. This Large Load 
Bilateral Transaction must be in place for each Obligation Procurement Period following the 
effective date of the Large Load’s Co-Located or Partner Resource Arrangement.  
This requirement ensures that the ICAP of the Associated Resource(s), which was caused 
to enter the ICAP market as a result of  the Large Load’s need to mitigate its adverse impacts to 
resource adequacy, continues to do so for the required term of the Co-Located or Partner 
Resource Arrangement, as further discussed below. Further, as the Smith Affidavit explains, the 
NYISO’s proposed rules will also ensure that Associated Resource(s) are not transient, and 
remain in the ICAP markets for a predetermined, minimum period of time.  
The NYISO’s Associated Resource arrangement construct and related rules are 
responsive to the Commission’s concern expressed in the Show Cause Order that “[w]ithout such 
a common and consistent understanding of entities’ responsibilities relevant to co-location 
arrangements [] we are concerned that these arrangements may be developed in a manner that is 
itself unjust and unreasonable or that may result in unjust and unreasonable rates for other 
customers[,]579 and encouragement for the NYISO to consider “what type of transmission service 
an Eligible Customer will need to take on behalf of the electrically proximate large load or large 
co-located load.”580  
The Show Cause Order’s suggested defined terminology of Large Load associating with 
“electrically proximate” supply. i.e., less than two substations away, is not well suited for 
 
578 See Proposed Services Tariff § 4.1.10.3. 
579 Show Cause Order at P 102. 
580 Show Cause Order at P 127 (emphasis added).

PDF page 109

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 109 
 
 
implementation in the NYISO.  Requiring an Associated Resource to be in the same Capacity 
Region as its associated Large Load is appropriate in light of the NYISO’s specific frameworks. 
This is because, during the interconnection process, when the deliverability of a new resource to 
be eligible to supply capacity is evaluated (i.e., the deliverability test is applied to projects 
seeking CRIS), the NYISO looks at the deliverability of the new Resource within the Capacity 
Region where that Resource is located.  Given that the Associate Resource is addressing the 
resource adequacy impact of the Large Load, it is appropriate to require that the Associated 
Resource be within the same Capacity Region as the Large Load in the NYISO . 
As such, the NYISO is proposing to allow Large Loads to partner or co-locate with 
Associated Resource(s) that are electrically located in the same Capacity Region as the Large 
Load.  This construct is more conducive to the way the NYISO’s resource adequacy 
requirements and ICAP market are structured.  And, as discussed in the Smith Affidavit, utilizing 
a Capacity Region construct in place of an electrical proximity approach is better suited to the 
NYISO’s markets and the configuration and topology of the NYS Transmission System.581   
The NYISO’s proposed Capacity Region requirement achieves the same objectives as the 
electrically proximate generation specifications discussed in the  Show Cause Order, including 
its expressed concern that “[i] the absence of Tariff provisions accounting for these operational 
dynamics when an interconnection customer’s generating facility is serving electrically 
proximate large loads or large co-located loads, new shovel-ready generating facilities may face 
unnecessary delays in reaching commercial operation under current generator interconnection 
processes and will be unable to serve the immediate demand of new large loads or large co-
located loads.”582  Allowing Large Loads to enter into arrangement with an Associated 
Resource(s) in the same Capacity Region, may avoid or lessen unnecessary delays in Large 
Loads properly reaching commercial operation.  Also, of note, in the Show Cause Order the 
Commission stated that it would consider “proposed revisions to this definition that ensure it is 
appropriately tailored to NYISO transmission system.”583

programs, and is treated the same as other firm Transmission Service customers, including being 
subject to the same curtailment priority and the NYISO’s emergency operating procedures.584 By 
contrast, Section 3.A.1.2 and the revised “Demand Side Resource”585 definition in Services

Load’s Associated Resource could exit the ICAP market, causing the first Large Load to 
reclassify as NFWS Load and causing it to lose its ability to participate in the markets as a 
Demand Side Resource. This, in turn, would cause the second Large Load to lose access to its 
Associated Resource, which was the first Large Load when it was operating as a Demand Side

Resource. Then, the second Large Load must also reclassify as NFWS Load. Such a mass

losing DSR eligibility, requiring them to withdraw their Demand Response capability from the 
ICAP, Energy, and Ancillary Services markets. Without such a prohibition in place, markets may 
inadvertently incentivize the creation of an interdependent web of Large Load FWS eligibilities 
(i.e., “daisy-chaining”) that could pose a risk to reliability and/or market stability.

provide for transmission services for Eligible Load and flexible large loads that are willing and 
able to limit their withdrawals.592   
H. 
DER Modeling Improvements and Baseline Enhancements 
The maximum physical injection capability for a Resource participating in an 
Aggregation is 20 MW, but Resources above 20 MW may participate in an Aggregation if the 
NYISO determines sufficient physical protection and control schemes exist to limit the injection 
capability of the Resource to 20 MW or less.593 
The NYISO proposes to prohibit Demand Side Resources whose Demand Response 
capability exceeds 20 MW from aggregating with other DER Facilities.594 Rather, Demand Side 
Resources exceeding 20 MW shall only be permitted to form an Aggregation of one (i.e., they 
shall not be permitted to form an Aggregation with other Distributed Energy Resources).595 
These revisions will provide necessary access and transparency into large DSR activity and will 
support safe and reliable real-time grid operations. This responds to the Commission’s directive 
in the Show Cause Order that the NYISO should address the lack of provisions addressing 
ongoing operational requirements for Transmission Customers taking Transmission Service on 
behalf of Large Loads in its tariffs.596 
These changes are grounded in working experience that NYISO has gained with the 
participation model over the past several years. The NYISO has found that, without such 
restrictions in place, the impacts of large Demand Side Resource dispatch on the transmission 
system becomes difficult to model and anticipate, and true impacts become difficult to deduce. 
The NYISO is concerned that the participation of Large Loads in the DER and Aggregation 
participation model as Demand Side Resources will exacerbate these modeling issues. The 
NYISO’s proposed enhancements will give the NYISO grid operators direct insight into the 
activity of individual, large, Demand Side Resources, making it easier to understand the impacts 
they are having on the transmission system and how the Demand Side Resource is currently 
operating. 
These rules have precedent within the DER and Aggregation participation model itself – 
other types of DER Facilities, such as small ESRs, may only participate in the DER and

Aggregation participation model if they are less than 20MW in size. The NYISO’s DER and 
Aggregation participation model rules made an exception for Demand Side Resources because, 
with the sunsetting of NYISO’s Demand Side Ancillary Services and Day Ahead Demand 
Response Programs, dispatchable Demand Side Resources had no other method of participation 
in the NYISO markets. Hence the DER and Aggregation participation model needed to support 
both standalone and aggregated Demand Side Resource participation use cases. These proposed 
revisions to the DER and Aggregation participation model do not restrict the model’s ability to 
handle both use cases. Rather, the revised rules more clearly define boundaries describing which 
Demand Side Resources should participate and therefore be modeled as individual Resources, vs 
those that may aggregate with other DER Facilities.   
The NYISO also proposes to require Demand Side Resources participating in the DER 
Participation Model to use “Meter Before/Meter After” baselining methodology to measure the 
amount of Demand Reduction provided in the first hour of Demand Side Resource dispatch.597  
The use of Meter Before/Meter After baselining will enhance the accuracy of the actual load 
reduction measurement.  Following that initial hour,  Aggregators will calculate a new baseline 
using the current Economic Customer Baseline Load (“ECBL”) methodology, and the ECBL 
methodology calculation will be used to measure DSR Demand Response for all subsequent 
intervals in the dispatch. This proposal responds to the Commission’s directive in the Show 
Cause Order that the NYISO should address the lack of provisions requiring the data necessary 
to ensure sufficient operational visibility nor establishes requirements for maintaining 
communication channels between the NYISO and Transmission Customers taking service on 
behalf of Large Loads.598   
I. 
Sunsetting the NYISO’s BTM:NG Resource Program 
The Show Cause Order made a preliminary finding that the NYISO’s Tariffs were unjust 
and unreasonable because “they allowed load with behind the meter generation to net its behind 
the meter generation against its load for purposes of calculating Transmission Service Charges 
for Host Loads configured in a BTM:NG Resource arrangement.”599  The NYISO acknowledges 
that its existing BTM:NG Resource program allows for such netting.  The NYISO takes no 
position at this time on whether this fact renders the existing program unjust and unreasonable. 
But the NYISO is nevertheless proposing to address the Commission’s concern here.    
The “netting” mechanism is an integral component of the BTM:NG Resource program. 
As such, the NYISO proposes to sunset the program, and transition its existing participants to 
another participation model.  Because the BTM:NG Resource program has relatively few 
participants and no recent enrollees600 it would be unreasonable to commit substantial resources 
to overhauling it in its current form at this time.  This is especially true given the need to 
prioritize work on many other large load reforms, including various others presented in this 
filing, that are more consequential.  It would therefore be preferable to replace the  BTM:NG

BTM:NG Resources have enrolled in recent years.

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese
