Resource program in the near future with an entirely new model that would address the 
Commission’s netting concern while also providing greater functionality and compatibility with 
the NYISO’s other Large Load enhancements.  
Accordingly, the NYISO seeks to sunset its existing BTM:NG program and to develop a 
replacement framework under which ancillary service, transmission, and other charges would be 
assessed on a Gross Load basis.  The NYISO would implement sunsetting by revising the 
definition of Behind-the-Meter Net Generation Resource in Section 2 of the Services Tariff to 
indicate that it will no longer be effective after May 1, 2029.  The NYISO would also end 
enrollment for new BTM:NG Resources as of February 1, 2027 which coincides with the 
proposed effective date of the LLIP and related tariff revisions.601   
The NYISO intends to work with the handful of current BTM:NG Resources to create 
individual transition plans that are suited for them in light of their operational characteristics and 
business models for continued participation in the ISO-administered markets.  As the Smith 
Affidavit notes, it will take time to develop individualized transition plans because the variability 
in BTM:NG Resource characteristics and capabilities makes a one-size fits all approach 
impractical.602  The NYISO will seek to complete the transition process before the existing 
BTM:NG program sunsets on May 1, 2029.   
J. 
Proposed Revisions to the NYISO’s Market Power Mitigation Measures  
i. 
Revisions to the “Buyer-Side” Capacity Market Power Mitigation 
Measures 
The NYISO is proposing to revise the definition of “Excluded Facility” in Section 23.2.1 
(Attachment H) to create two new exclusions from its existing buyer-side capacity market power 
mitigation measures.   
The BSM Rules apply to new capacity resources entering the ICAP Auctions in New 
York City and the Lower Hudson Valley, i.e., the NYISO’s “Mitigated Capacity Zones.”  
Traditionally, any such resource that did not qualify for an exemption was required to offer its 
capacity into the Spot Market Auctions at a price at or above the applicable offer floor and to 
then continue to offer at or above that price until its capacity clears twelve, not necessarily 
consecutive, monthly Spot Market Auction. 
In 2022, the Commission accepted a substantial new exclusion from the BSM Rules.603  
New capacity resources that were deemed to serve the objectives of New York State’s Climate 
Leadership and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) were no longer subject to the BSM Rules 
partly in consideration of New York State’s reserved authority under the F PA to determine its 
resource mix.  The NYISO demonstrated that the CLCPA exclusion was consistent with 
 
601 See Proposed Services Tariff Section 4.2. 
602 Smith Affidavit at P 103. 
603 N.Y. Indep. Sys. Operator, Inc., 179 FERC ¶ 61,102 (2022) (“CLCPA Exclusion Order”).

PDF page 114

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 114 
 
 
Commission precedent that market power mitigation rules must balance investor and consumer 
interests by avoiding both over-mitigation and artificial price suppression.604    
The two exclusions that  the NYISO is proposing now are considerably narrower in scope 
than the CLCPA exclusion.  Both are necessary to update and clarify the BSM Rules to account 
for issues presented by the influx of new types of Large Loads. And both will reasonably guard 
against the risks of over- and under-mitigation.  
The NYISO seeks to establish an exclusion for new generation that is co-located with 
Large Loads or that enters a partnership with Large Loads so that the Large Loads may receive 
Firm Withdrawal Service.  Such generation must also obtain CRI S through the NYISO’s 
interconnection processes.  The exclusion would recognize that the NYISO has proposed to 
require arrangements with generation to address a given Large Loads’ resource adequacy 
impacts as determined through the LLIP.   
As is discussed in the Altman Affidavit, the NYISO currently faces unprecedented near-
term resource adequacy challenges.  The purpose of requiring Co-Located/Partnered 
Arrangements is to offset the substantial resource adequacy impacts created by new Large Loads.  
This is an important policy objective that I believe justifies a new exclusion from the BSM 
Rules. Today’s resource adequacy challenges certainly did not exist and were not accounted for 
when the BSM Rules were first devised or as they evolved over time. 
In addition, it is highly unlikely that Large Load projects and Associated Resources 
would be jointly developed as part of capacity market price suppression schemes. Development 
times are much shorter for Large Loads than for generation.  The risk of Large Load projects 
being effectively used to cause artificial price suppression is certainly lower than it was for the 
kinds of subsidized generation investments that the BSM Rules, and comparable measures in 
other regions, were originally devised to prevent.  Creating an exclusion for Co-
Located/Partnered Generation should therefore not reasonably be expected to result in under-
mitigation.  
During the NYISO stakeholder process that preceded this filing, some stakeholders 
suggested that the new exclusion should be modified to include a “right-sizing” limitation, i.e., 
that it should not apply to the extent that Co-Located/Partnered Generation was materially larger 
than the associated Large Load.  The Newton Affidavit explains that these hypothetical scenarios 
presented as justifications for right-sizing do not appear to represent realistic mechanisms for the 
exercise of buyer-side market power under current market conditions.  
Furthermore, many of the resource technology types referenced by stakeholders are 
already Excluded Facilities on other grounds and thus are not subject to the BSM Rules under the 
currently effective tariff.  The NYISO is not proposing to change any of the existing exclusion 
rules in this proceeding.  For the limited resource types that remain subject to the BSM Rules, 
 
604 Id. at P 39.

PDF page 115

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 115 
 
 
there does not appear to be a credible near-term market power concern that would necessitate 
adding a right-sizing rule or other restrictions.  
The NYISO also seeks to introduce an exclusion for any proposed supply-side solution 
responding to a reliability need identified by the NYISO in a STAR or RNA that is found to be a 
viable and sufficient response to that need. 
In the past the Commission sometimes rejected reliability-based exceptions to capacity 
market power mitigation rules. But circumstances have changed greatly in recent years.  This 
includes rapid growth across new types of loads that was not anticipated at the time of the 
Commission’s earlier rulings.  Reliability conditions may not yet be as severe in New York as 
they are becoming in certain other regions. But the challenges facing the NYISO are serious and 
must be addressed. 
The NYISO is proposing that once a capacity resource in a Mitigated Capacity Zone  
obtains one of the two new exclusions that the exclusion would not be rescinded. This is 
consistent with how the existing CLCPA exclusion is implemented. 
Although it is proposing to add a new exclusion to its tariff, the NYISO would not turn a 
blind eye toward potential buyer-side market power abuses.  As the NYISO stated when it 
proposed the existing exclusion for CLCPA resources, the NYISO “will continue to monitor and 
identify any relevant market behaviors or developments that could constitute abuses of buyer-
side market power. If the NYISO were to identify any such exercise of buyer-side market power, 
it would take all appropriate and timely actions to address such abuse and protect against 
unreasonable capacity market outcomes.”605  This could include proposing prospectively 
effective tariff changes that would prevent an inappropriate exclusion from being granted before 
final investment decisions are made.  
Similarly, with respect to the new exclusion(s) that the NYISO is proposing now, the 
NYISO’s Market Mitigation and Analysis Department  (“MMA”) would continue to monitor the 
capacity market for potential exercises of buyer-side market power.  MMA will monitor new 
capacity entry, market outcomes, and other information that could indicate the exercise of buyer-
side market power.  
The interconnection process, stakeholder process, and public visibility associated with 
large load infrastructure projects provide significant additional transparency – and time to act if 
required.  Projects with the attributes that have concerned some stakeholders, e.g., , an 
Associated Resource far larger than its associated load, would likely be identified and reviewed 
well in advance of entering service.  This would give the NYISO, its independent Market 
Monitoring Unit, and stakeholders sufficient time to assess potential concerns and responses, 
including potential tariff changes if needed to close loopholes and prevent abuses in individual 
cases.

The NYISO also proposes to revise Section 23 of the Services Tariff (Attachment H) to

and/or a Generator to identify physical withholding, economic withholding, or uneconomic

The NYISO’s proposed changes to its market power mitigation measures are not a direct 
response to any finding in the Show Cause Order.  But they are just and reasonable revisions to 
account for the attributes and impacts of Large Loads.  
V. 
THE NYISO’S PROPOSALS BUILD ON THE NYISO’S ESTABLISHED 
TRANSMISISON MODEL AND MARKET STRUCTURES AND DO NOT NEED 
TO INCORPORATE THE PJM CO-LOCATION ORDER’S TRANSMISSION 
SERVICES  
The Commission has repeatedly determined, the NYISO’s financial reservation based 
transmission service (“Financial Transmission Service”) rules are consistent with or superior to 
the Order No. 888 pro forma service offerings.610  For the reasons explained below, the NYISO’s 
existing Financial Transmission Service model represents the best, the most practically 
implementable, and the most flexible way for the NYISO to serve Large Loads, including co-
located Large Loads and flexible Large Loads.  In this Part, the NYISO explains why its 
Financial Transmission Service continues to produce more efficient solutions than the Order No. 
888 pro forma physical transmission service options, and why it should serve as the foundation 
for the new withdrawal services proposed in this filing.  The NYISO also explains the reasons

why it is not proposing to adopt the PJM Co-Location Order’s611 forms of transmission service in

A. 
The NYISO’s Financial Transmission Service Model Enables the NYISO to 
Maximize the Use of the Transmission System Efficiently and to Support 
Flexible Service for Large Loads  
The NYISO does not accept or facilitate reservations of physical transmission service 
within the NYCA.  Instead, the NYISO offers “financial reservation” based transmission service 
where LBMP energy markets are used to manage congestion and operate bid-based markets.612 
In 1999,613 the Commission accepted the NYISO’s proposed Point-to-Point (“PTP”) and 
Network Integration Transmission Service (“NITS”) provisions in the OATT, which are 
substantially different from the physical based pro forma transmission services, finding the 
NYISO’s variations to be consistent with or superior to the pro forma OATT.614  The 
Commission has repeatedly found the NYISO’s financial reservation model to be just and 
reasonable in various contexts.615  
 
Physical transmission rights are different from the NYISO’s Commission-accepted 
“financial” reservation regime that (a) bundles transmission service with Energy purchases and 
deliveries, and (b) does not provide for Market Participants to reserve and hold transfer 
capability, but rather makes access to, beneficial use of and the capability of its transmission 
system available through scheduling each day in its Day-Ahead and Real-Time Markets.616 
Transmission service within the NYCA is scheduled “implicitly” when Resources and External 
 
611 PJM Interconnection, L.L.C., 193 FERC ¶ 61,217 (2025). 
612The original 1998 proposal to establish the NYISO provided for a single form of financial reservation- 
based transmission service.  However, in 1999, the Commission required the NYISO’s sponsors to file an OATT 
that included two kinds of transmission service but allowed them to propose changes from the pro forma OATT to 
reflect how the financial nature of the NYISO’s model differs from the physical reservation-based transmission 
service.  See Cent. Hudson Gas & Elec. Corp., 88 FERC ¶ 61,138, 61,382 (1999) (accepting the revised financial 
transmission reservation model for the NYISO.)  For the reasons explained below, the NYISO’s financial 
reservation system bridges the gap between the traditional “point-to-point” and “network” transmission service 
offerings.  As further detailed herein, the NYISO’s financial transmission service method provides greater flexibility 
to customers than traditional point-to-point and traditional network service do and, consistent with the proposed 
modifications to accommodate “non-firm” service to Large Loads that are described in this filing, the NYISO’s 
financial reservation transmission model can accommodate Large Loads that are not eligible to receive firm 
transmission service at times when the transmission system is significantly congested or available Energy is limited.

PDF page 118

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese

Page 118 
 
 
Transactions receive Energy schedules from the NYISO, and NYCA Loads are served without 
the need to formally schedule transmission service.  
 
The Financial Transmission Service that customers select and the NYISO has provided 
since its inception in 2000 most closely resembles an improved, more flexible version of pro 
forma NITS which effectively designates all NYCA Resources  to serve all NYCA Loads with 
administrative efficiency. The NYISO commits and dispatches all NYCA Resources based on 
their economic offers, accounting for congestion on its transmission network, to serve all NYCA 
Loads (plus scheduled Export Transactions) at the least production cost.  The transmission 
service that customers select, and the NYISO provides, is referred to as Firm Point-to-Point 
Transmission Service.617 As the Commission noted in the Show Cause Order,618 in the twenty-
six years since the NYISO commenced operations, it has never “received a formal request from a 
transmission customer for NITS.” 
 
An internal Bilateral Transaction provides a financial method for a NYCA Generator to 
contractually serve a NYCA Load.  The Point of Injection (“POI”) for an internal Bilateral 
Transaction is the Generator Bus where the Energy the Generator produces is made available to 
the NYISO. The Point of Withdrawal (“POW”) is the Zonal Load Bus for the Load Zone to 
which the Generator’s Energy is being financially “delivered.”619  To the extent the participating 
Generator is producing Energy, the “cost” of obtaining transmission service is limited to paying 
for transmission congestion and losses between the POI and the POW. If the MWh scheduled in 
the Bilateral Transaction exceed the source Generator’s output, the additional MWh required to 
serve the scheduled Bilateral Transaction are purchased by the Generator from the LBMP market 
based on the LBMP at the source Generator’s location.620  Compared to “physical” transmission 
service, market efficiency is improved by providing “financial” transmission service using 
Bilateral Transactions because the scheduling of a Bilateral Transaction does not disrupt the 
NYISO’s economic, least production cost commitment of Resources to serve all NYCA Load 
(plus Export Transactions). Unlike a physical transmission service option, the NYISO’s financial 
scheduling of Bilateral Transactions does not force the commitment of a “source” Generator to 
serve the Load if that Generator is not an efficient source of economic Energy based on the Bid it 
submitted. 
 
617 OATT Sections 16.3.1 through 16.3.4.2 address Bilateral Transactions and Wheels Through the NYCA. 
Possibly, customers have only ever requested Firm PTP Service by default given that, under the NYISO’s model, 
PTP service offers all of the benefits of NITS in an administratively efficient manner and thus the two services are 
largely equivalent; however, it is also possible that customers only select PTP because the limited set of transactions 
that need Transmission Service to support them (i.e., Bilateral Transactions and Wheel-Through Transactions) are 
readily scheduled on a point-to-point basis. In any case, for over twenty-six years NITS has never been formally 
requested or provided in the NYCA. 
618 See N.Y. Indep. Sys. Operator, Inc., 195 FERC ¶ 61,216 at P 20 fn. 69 (2026) (“[The NYISO] has never 
provided or received a formal request from a transmission customer for NITS.”). 
619 NYCA Generators are paid for the Energy they produce based on the LBMP at their Generator Bus.  
NYCA Loads pay for the Energy they consume based on the Zonal LBMP calculated for the Load Zone in which 
the Load is located.   
620 Energy Imbalance Service for Generators serving Bilateral Transactions is addressed in Rate Schedule 4 
(Section 6.4) of the OATT.  At times when the “source” Generator for a Bilateral Transaction is not the least cost 
producer of Energy based on its economic Bids, the LBMP Market will provide the Energy at a lower cost from 
other sources, improving efficiency for all involved.

In the two and a half decades since the NYISO commenced operations, Transmission 
Customers have only ever used the NYISO’s financial based PTP Transmission Service. The 
NITS provided for under the NYISO OATT has never been used.  As OATT Section 4.2.3 
indicates, the NYISO would need to develop substantial new software functionality in order to 
implement NITS.  One characteristic of the pro forma version of NITS is the ability of a 
Transmission Customer to schedule service from one or more designated Generators to one or 
more designated Loads.  The universe of Resources available for economic commitment and 
dispatch by the NYISO is larger and more diverse than an individual Transmission Customer’s 
set of designated network resources under the pro forma OATT.  The financial transmission 
service that NYISO provides is superior to pro forma NITS because the NYISO dispatches all 
available Resources based on their economic offers and accounting for the capability of the 
transmission system, to serve NYCA Load at least production cost.   
 
Currently, under the NYISO’s model, non-firm transmission service is not available 
because it is not needed within the framework of the NYISO’s Financial Transmission Service 
Model. Initially, the NYISO offered Non-Firm PTP service for internal Transactions, which was 
described as being service for which a Transmission Customer is not willing to pay Congestion 
and was made available on a stand-alone basis for one hour periods.621 The NYISO has not 
offered Non-Firm PTP service for internal Transactions since March 2011.622 Additionally, Non-
Firm Point-To-Point Transmission Service for External Transactions was eliminated in August 
2014.623 As the Harvey Affidavit notes, the non-firm transmission service option that previously 
existed under the NYISO OATT was hardly ever used.624  The NYISO’s transmission model 
lends itself to providing Loads with the flexibility to structure its service in a manner that 
reasonably aligns with its operational dynamics, and is made available to Loads on a relatively 
short timeline.  
B. 
The NYISO’s Financial Reservation Model Promotes Economic Efficiency in 
the Use of the Transmission System and Resources  
Under the NYISO’s model, the most economically efficient use of the transmission 
system and generation resources is achieved within the context of the LBMP energy markets.625 
The NYISO’s congestion management framework promotes efficient use of the transmission 
grid through the Security Constrained Unit Commitment (“SCUC”) mechanism conducted in the 
 
621 The NYISO’s original Non-Firm Point-to-Point Transmission Services rules were previously found in 
Section 3.2 of the NYISO OATT.  
622 New York Independent System Operator, Inc., Letter Order, Docket No. ER11-2459-000 (Feb. 10, 
2011); see also NYISO’s Proposed Tariff Clarifications Regarding the Bidding for and Scheduling of Energy in New 
York’s LBMP Market and the Scheduling of Transmission Service in the New York Control Area, Docket No. ER11-
2459-000 at p. 15 (December 21, 2010) (“the NYISO proposes to affirmatively indicate that Non-Firm Transmission 
Service is unavailable for internal Transactions”).   
623 New York Independent System Operator, Inc., Letter Order, Docket No. ER14-2623-000 (Oct. 8, 2014) 
624 See Harvey Affidavit at P 23. 
625 As discussed supra, under the NYISO’s approach, the Transmission Customer bears the financial 
responsibility for the marginal congestion cost associated with its own transactions.

PDF page 120

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 120 
 
 
Day-Ahead Market (“DAM”) and the Real-Time Commitment (“RTC”) and Real-Time Dispatch 
(“RTD”) conducted in the Real-Time Market (“RTM”).  
While PJM also administers co-optimized day-ahead and real-time energy and ancillary 
services markets and its transmission model involves Financial Transmission Rights somewhat 
akin to the NYISO’s TCCs, the NYISO’s transmission model is materially different from PJM’s 
because the NYISO’s financial reservation-based model does not involve any physical 
reservations for transmission service. 
Through the NYISO’s DAM and RTM software, internal New York generation resources 
compete, including with External Transactions, to be economically awarded an Energy schedule, 
which includes the necessary transmission service and ramp schedule. Additionally, the NYISO 
continuously redispatches the grid on a security-constrained, least-cost basis in order to 
maximize the efficient use of the system. As such, the total production cost to serve Load is 
minimized by the NYISO’s SCUC, RTC and RTD, which provides significant savings to Loads 
compared to a dispatch that matches Generator output to the demand of a specific sub-set of 
Loads.  
NYISO’s market constructs, specifically the Transmission Congestion Contract market, 
also provides a pathway to manage congestion cost (i.e., the Transmission Usage Charge 
associated with firm transmission) for instances where ex-ante price certainty is valuable. TCCs 
provide a voluntary financial hedge against Day-Ahead congestion costs without conferring a 
physical scheduling priority or altering the NYISO’s security-constrained dispatch. This 
separation permits customers to obtain greater cost certainty while preserving efficient, 
nondiscriminatory access to transmission capability for all market participants.  
The financial reservation model also accommodates materially different Large Load 
operating characteristics without creating administratively defined transmission priorities. A 
Large Load that can reduce withdrawals during constrained conditions may express that 
flexibility through its scheduling parameters and applicable operational requirements, while a 
Large Load that requires uninterrupted withdrawals (and whose interconnection supports such 
un-interrupted withdrawals) remains exposed to the costs associated with that service. The model 
therefore preserves a direct relationship between the service a customer values, the flexibility it 
provides, and the congestion costs it causes, rather than socializing those costs or embedding 
them in a new class of physical transmission rights. This supports efficient customer decisions 
regarding location, consumption, onsite supply, contractual hedging, and willingness to curtail. 
The model is also adaptable as system conditions and Large Load consumption change 
over time. Financial schedules and locational settlements can reflect the actual hourly—and, in 
real time, interval-specific—effects of a Large Load’s withdrawals, avoiding the need to 
establish a static physical reservation based on forecasts that may prove inaccurate or operating 
assumptions that may later change. That adaptability is especially valuable for Large Loads 
whose development timelines, utilization levels, and operating profiles may be uncertain. It 
reduces the risk that scarce transmission capability will be stranded or inefficiently reserved, 
protects other customers from subsidizing uneconomic uses of the system, and permits Large

PDF page 121

Hon. Debbie-Anne A. Reese  
 
 
November 16, 2026 
Page 121 
 
 
Loads to obtain service commensurate with their demonstrated needs and willingness to bear the 
resulting costs. 
Importantly, the financial reservation model does not substitute congestion pricing for the 
recovery of Large Load-specific interconnection or upgrade costs. Rather, it preserves efficient 
system operations while allowing separate, targeted tariff provisions to address cost 
responsibility, readiness, and reliability concerns without converting those protections into 
physical scheduling preferences that could distort dispatch or strand transmission capability. 
As such, the NYISO’s financial reservation system is beneficial because it enables the 
NYISO to efficiently use the full capability of the transmission system to arrive at a least 
production cost solution to serve NYCA Load using available Resources.626 All desired uses of 
the transmission system are scheduled to the extent that customers are willing to pay congestion 
charges. 
C. 
There Is No Need for the NYISO to Adopt the Transmission Services 
Established by the PJM Co-Location Order 
The Show Cause Order made a preliminary finding that: 
NYISO’s Tariff appears to be unjust and unreasonable because it does not include 
transmission services that reflect Eligible Customers taking transmission service 
on behalf of Eligible Load that are willing and able to limit their use of the 
transmission system under certain conditions.  NYISO should explain whether the 
Tariff remains just and reasonable without the transmission services described by 
the Commission in the PJM Co-Location Order, with regional variation as 
appropriate, being available to Eligible Customers taking transmission service on 
behalf of Eligible Load, or propose appropriate Tariff revisions.  Specifically, 
NYISO should explain whether the Tariff remains just and reasonable without (1) 
an interim non-firm network transmission service while Network Upgrades are 
being constructed (i.e., interim NITS) and (2) permanent firm and non-firm 
contract demand transmission services (transmission service up to a specified 
MW quantity, i.e., the contract level, on a firm or non-firm basis).627   
The NYISO is not proposing to adopt the three transmission services described in the 
PJM Co-Location Order (the “PJM Co-Location Order Services”).   
The NYISO notes that the Commission issued a Show Cause Order for the California 
Independent System Operator Corporation (“CAISO Show Cause Order”) at the same time as the

it did for the NYISO.628  The CAISO Show Cause Order recognized that the “CAISO does not 
offer traditional Order No. 888 network and point-to-point transmission services.”629  It invited 
CAISO to “explain whether, given that CAISO does not offer the transmission services required 
by Order No. 888, CAISO’s existing transmission service framework addresses the concerns 
described herein, or explain what specific changes to its existing transmission service framework 
would effectively provide the same range of transmission service options for flexible large loads 
as in the PJM Co-Location Order.”630   
The Show Cause Order acknowledges in passing that the NYISO also provides non-
traditional transmission services.631  But the Commission did not expressly invite the  NYISO to 
justify its “existing transmission service framework” as it did the CAISO.  Nevertheless, the 
Commission should recognize that the NYISO’s financial transmission model is functionally the 
same the CAISO’s.  As discussed above and in the Harvey Affidavit, the NYISO, like the 
CAISO, does not offer “traditional Order No. 888 network and point-to-point transmission 
services,” does not make physical long-term transmission capacity reservations available, and 
“does not provide a formal application process for transmission service.”632  The Commission 
should therefore be every bit as open to the NYISO’s transmission service proposals in this filing 
as it has indicated it will be to alternatives offered by the CAISO.633  
 
Dr. Scott Harvey, a leading expert on the NYISO’s and CAISO’s transmission models, 
market designs, and market software confirms the close similarities between the NYISO’s and 
CAISO’s transmission regimes.634  Dr. Harvey emphasizes that, “NYISO’s existing firm service 
and proposed new firm and non-firm withdrawal services address the need for tariff provisions 
covering large loads that are able to curtail use of the transmission system when needed to avoid 
adverse reliability and market impacts.”635  Dr. Harvey also states that “the NYISO’s proposed 
Firm Withdrawal Service/Non-Firm Withdrawal Service framework is a reasonable and efficient 
approach to serving large loads able to curtail load in real-time based on system conditions that is 
compatible with and an effective application of the established NYISO financial reservation 
model based on financial transmission rights and economic bids and security constrained 
economic dispatch.”636  Dr. Harvey sees, “no need for the NYISO to adopt interim non-firm 
NITS or contract demand services that were designed with a different region and different tariff 
design in mind.  NYISO’s proposed Firm and Non-Firm withdrawal services will bring

comparable (or greater) benefits and are more compatible with the NYISO’s financial

In addition, Dr. Harvey demonstrates that there are various transmission cost recovery 
and cost allocation differences between the NYISO’s transmission model and those used in other 
regions that would further complicate any effort to implement the PJM Co-Location Order 
Services in the NYISO.  Dr. Harvey emphasizes that the NYISO’s decision not to adopt the PJM 
Co-Location Order Services is “superior to a large load tariff based on interim non-firm NITS 
and firm and non-firm contract demand service because the NYISO approach avoids the need for 
a completely unnecessary change in the NYISO’s wholesale transmission cost recovery design 
from one based on megawatt hours of withdrawals to one based on megawatt reservations . . .  
.”638 The Harvey Affidavit provides a detailed review of the relevant distinctions that support his 
conclusion.639 
 
As explained above, the NYISO’s currently offers a single form of firm financial 
reservation transmission service that operates as an improved, more flexible version of pro forma 
NITS that does not subject customers to various restrictions that exist under the physical 
reservation version of NITS.  Moreover, the NITS procedures under the NYISO OATT have 
never actually been used, even though they are theoretically available.  There is thus no reason to 
introduce a non-firm interim version of NITS that the Commission envisioned as a bridge to firm 
NITS service when firm NITS service is not actually used in the NYISO.  
 
Similarly, the NYISO proposes to offer its new Non-Firm Withdrawal Service as an 
option to allow new Large Loads to connect to the grid faster and without the potential delays 
required to mitigate adverse impacts to resource adequacy and/or transmission security.  It is a 
voluntary Transmission Service option that new Large Loads are permitted to choose provided 
however that they comply with the applicable terms and conditions described in Part IV.B.ii 
above.  Reintroducing a new form of non-firm service, i.e., Non-Firm Withdrawal Service, to the 
options available in the NYISO is a more than sufficient substitute for interim non-firm NITS 
and the two contract demand services.  
Furthermore, the NYISO is concerned that attempting to add the PJM Co-Located Order 
Services to the NYISO’s model would be detrimental to the economic efficiencies achieved by 
the NYISO’s existing market constructs.  The result could be reducing the efficiency of the 
NYISO’s least production cost commitment and dispatch system and to increase the overall cost 
of serving Load in New York, as further explained below.  The NYISO does not believe that this 
would be a just and reasonable outcome given the lack of a rationale for adopting the PJM Co-
Location Order Services in NYISO.  
During the extensive stakeholder process that culminated in this filing, no stakeholder 
argued that the NYISO should adopt any of the three PJM Co-Location Order Services in lieu of 
NYISO’s proposed Withdrawal Services.  The NYISO believes that this fact is further evidence

that the PJM Co-Location Order Service would serve no useful purpose in the NYISO, no matter 
their potential benefits in PJM or other regions.640    
 
Finally, the NYISO understands that PJM has indicated it could take several years to 
implement the PJM Co-Location Order Services.  PJM initially proposed a June 1, 2029 
implementation date even though the services are native to PJM and were designed to 
complement PJM’s established rules and procedures.  By contrast, the NYISO would effectively 
be starting from scratch and would have to make many more software and tariff changes if it 
were required to integrate the PJM Co-Location Order Services into its model.  Adopting the 
PJM services would also very likely require the NYISO to take extensive measures to comply 
with numerous North American Energy Standards Board requirements that do not currently 
apply to it.  In short, adapting the PJM Co-Location Order Services to fit the NYISO would all 
but certainly be more expensive and time-consuming for the NYISO than for PJM.  By contrast, 
NYISO’s proposed Withdrawal Services would provide comparable or greater benefits and do so 
more quickly, i.e., by the NYISO’s proposed June 1, 2028 effective date for its Large Load 
market participation tariff revisions.   
VI. 
THE NYTOS AND THE NTOS ARE ADDRESSING CERTAIN SHOW CAUSE 
ORDER ISSUES IN  SEPARATE FPA SECTION 205 FILINGS 
The Show Cause Order recognized that certain large load issues involve matters over 
which New York’s transmission-owning jurisdictional utilities retain unilateral Section 205 filing 
rights.641  The Commission gave the transmission owners an opportunity to address those issues 
in their own FPA Section 205 filings.  Subsequently, the Abeyance Order granted both the 
NYTOs and the NTOs additional time to make such filings. 
The NYISO understands that the NYTOs will make a concurrent filing to address several 
Show Cause Order issues that they assert are subject to their reserved filing rights under the 
ISO/TO Agreement.  This will involve a number of revisions to NYISO OATT Section 5 
(Special Provisions for Retail Access) to address large load-related rate issues that were not 
contemplated when Section 5 was written.  The NYISO also expects that the NYTOs will 
propose some modifications to proposed Attachment LL and OATT Section 1. The NYTOs may 
make additional Section 205 filings to address rate and cost recovery matters after the NYPSC 
provides guidance on state-jurisdictional issues in its ongoing large load proceedings, i.e., Case 
No. 26-E-0045.  
The NYTOs will also propose four new two-party pro forma agreements that would be 
added to the NYISO OATT.  Specifically: (i) a Network Upgrade Cost Recovery Agreement; (ii) 
a Security Agreement; (iii) a Facilities Study Agreement; and (iv) a Large Load Interconnection 
Agreement.  The NYISO would not be a party to any of these arrangements.

VII. 
THE FPA SECTION 205 STANDARD OF REVIEW AND SEVERABILITY 
The Commission has explained that “[u]nder FPA section 205, the Commission is limited 
to considering the filing before it, . . . .”643  Federal courts have similarly noted that “[w]hen 
acting on a public utility’s rate filing under section 205, the Commission undertakes ‘an 
essentially passive and reactive role’ and restricts itself to evaluating the confined proposal.”644  
Courts have further acknowledged that “FERC has interpreted its authority to review rates under 
[the FPA] as limited to an inquiry into whether the rates proposed by a utility are reasonable—
and not to extend to determining whether a proposed rate schedule is more or less reasonable 
than alternative rate designs.”645 The Commission “is not required to choose the best solution, 
only a reasonable one.”646 
Adhering to these precedents and principles is especially appropriate in this proceeding.  
The NYISO’s proposal addresses a large number of operationally and legally complex questions 
in the context of the resource adequacy challenges facing New York.  This included considering 
jurisdictional issues, particularly the NYPSC’s views as a state regulator in the process of 
devising its own large load interconnection policies.  The NYISO’s proposal also had to be 
developed exceptionally quickly while balancing the interests of many parties in order to obtain 
super-majority stakeholder support. The proposal should therefore be judged on whether it 
produces just, reasonable, not unduly discriminatory, and lawful rules – not on whether it will 
result in the best imaginable solution in every instance.   
The Commission should also assess the NYISO’s proposals in light of the NYISO-
specific conditions and circumstances that shaped it.  As Chairman Swett noted in her 
concurrence to the Show Cause Order, the Commission “has long allowed different regional

transmission organizations to follow different rules, in recognition of regional variations . . . .”647  
The NYISO is starting from a different place, with different market, transmission, and 
interconnection rules that several other ISOs and RTOs.  It should not be a surprise, and is 
entirely reasonable, that NYISO would develop distinctive proposals to address the Show Cause 
Order’s concerns.  What matters is that the NYISO’s proposals are just, reasonable, and not 
unduly discriminatory on their own merits.  
Each element of this proposal is just and reasonable. Nevertheless, in the event the 
Commission deems it necessary, and to help facilitate the Commission’s acceptance of this filing 
without delay, NYISO provides notice that it consents to the Commission’s exercise of authority 
to modify and/or sever any element of the proposed OATT and Services Tariff language to the 
extent necessary and permitted under FPA section 205 and NRG Power Marketing, LLC v. 
FERC,648 where such modification and/or severance is consistent with the NYISO’s overarching 
objectives in this filing. While the each of the reforms proposed in this filing is just and 
reasonable and act together to advance important objectives, they may also be considered 
separately. In particular, the NYISO consents to making any clarifications to its proposed tariff 
revisions that the Commission considers necessary to confirm that Firm and Non-Firm 
Withdrawal Services are Commission-jurisdictional transmission services.  
VIII. STAKEHOLDER APPROVAL 
The NYISO used the additional time provided by the Abeyance Order to conduct an 
expedited, extensive, and ultimately successful stakeholder process.  The NYISO held numerous 
stakeholder meetings and made presentations describing its evolving proposals on July 23, 
August 13, August 20, September 1, September 8, September 14, September 22, October 1, 
October 7, and October 14.  As the Smith Affidavit notes, this was not a straightforward effort 
but an iterative, intense, and thorough process.  The NYISO’s proposals are the product of 
countless hours of internal coordination amongst NYISO business units and stakeholder 
deliberations under significant time pressure.  The proposals have evolved and matured 
substantially since they were first proposed as a direct result of stakeholder feedback.  
A near-final version of the NYISO’s proposal was discussed at a joint meeting of the 
NYISO’s Business Issues Committee and Operating Committee on October 21.  Several 
changes, most notably the addition of the State Exempt Large Load Projects rules, were made in 
response to comments presented at joint meeting.  Subsequently, the NYISO’s final proposal was 
approved by the stakeholder Management Committee on October 28 by a 100% vote with 
abstentions.649  The proposal was then approved for filing by the NYISO’s independent Board of 
Directors on November 16.

IX. 
REQUESTED EFFECTIVE DATES 
The NYISO respectfully requests a February 1, 2027 effective date for proposed OATT 
Attachment LL, i.e., the new LLIP, all new defined terms referenced in Attachment LL, Section 
3A of the OATT, all other OATT and Services Tariff revisions directly related to Attachment LL 
that are discussed in Parts II and III of this transmittal letter, and the BTM:NG sunsetting-related 
revisions referenced in Part IV.I above.  Because February 1 is less than 120 days after the date 
of this filing there is no need for a demonstration of good cause to support these proposed 
effective dates under Section 35.3 of the Commission’s regulations.  The NYISO expects to be 
ready to commence the optional pre-application process described proposed Attachment LL 
Section 44.5 on February 1.   
The requested February 1 effective date for the new Large Load Integration Procedures 
also means that the NYISO’s currently effective large load interconnection procedures in OATT 
Sections 3.9 and 4.5 will continue to be in effect for the entirety of the comment period.  This is 
more than eight months after the issuance of the Show Cause Order which will accommodate 
existing commercial arrangements. 
The NYISO is proposing to make its proposed tariff revisions related to its new market 
participation provisions for Large Loads, i.e., the revisions described in Part IV (other than Part 
IV.I) of this transmittal letter, effective on June 1, 2028.  There is good cause for allowing these 
provisions to become effective more than 120 days after this filing.  No Large Loads are 
expected to have completed the new LLIP, and thus to be able to utilize the new market 
participation provisions, until June 1, 2028 at the earliest.    
Finally, the NYISO’s is proposing to make its revision to Section 2 of the Services Tariff, 
which would effectuate the sunsetting of the existing BTM:NG program, effective on May 1, 
2029.  There is good cause for an effective date more than 120 days in the future because it will 
allow time for the handful of existing BTM:NG market participants to transition to alternative 
participation models.

The Abeyance Order stated that, “[i]f  NYISO or Transmission Owners submit an FPA 
section 205 filing addressing the Commission’s preliminary findings in the Show Cause Order 
on or before November 16, 2026, then the obligation of the respondent(s) who have made such 
an FPA section 205 filing to file a response to the Show Cause Order will be suspended, and this 
proceeding will continue to be held in abeyance pending further Commission directive in this

proceeding.”650  The NYISO therefore requests that the Commission continue to hold the 
proceedings in Docket No. EL26-69-000, and the NYISO’s obligation to submit an answer to the

In addition, the NYISO requests that if the Commission were to require the NYISO to 
make additional tariff revisions before accepting the NYISO’s proposals under FPA Section 205 
that it hold the Show Cause proceedings in further abeyance for a reasonable time to give the 
NYISO an opportunity to prepare the necessary filing.    
XI. 
REQUEST FOR EXTENDED COMMENT PERIOD 
The NYISO respectfully requests that the Commission extend the standard 21-day 
comment period for FPA section 205 filings by one week.  The Commission has often granted 
extended comment periods for major filings that encompass numerous complex issues and tariff 
revisions that are likely to have substantial economic and reliability implications.  At the same 
time, the NYISO recognizes the importance of the issues addressed by this filing and 
understands the Commission’s desire to address large load issues rapidly.  The NYISO is 
mindful that the Commission has denied extension requests in certain recent involving large load 
matters.651  Accordingly, the NYISO submits that a brief one-week extension to the standard 
period so that comments would be due on December 14 instead of December 7 is reasonable 
given the number, complexity, and importance of the  proposals included in this filing.  Such an 
extension would also make a reasonable allowance for the comment period encompassing the 
Thanksgiving holiday. Because the earliest effective date requested in this filing is February 1, 
2027, i.e., two weeks beyond the default sixty-day period, a one-week extension would leave the 
Commission with slightly more time to review this filing than if the standard comment and 
notice periods applied.
